**The cache comparison, and why it is not enough on its own.** `Connector.learnResolved` records a peer's resolved remote id under its short key (`c.remotes`) and reports the upgrade only when that replaces a *different* id already cached this run - almost always the placeholder a moment earlier in the same run. That alone misses the case that matters most for an already-duplicated store: a placeholder created in an earlier run, with nothing cached after a helper restart for the comparison to catch. `sync.go`'s `listDialogs`, which runs on every connect, so also covers every reconnect, closes that gap by also asking unconditionally, for every dialog it lists, to upgrade the placeholder shape that peer *would* have had (`placeholderRemoteID`) into the one it just resolved - regardless of what, if anything, was cached before. Asking is cheap (one indexed lookup) and always safe to repeat: `UpgradeConversationRemoteID` reports `domain.ErrNotFound` and changes nothing when no conversation actually has that placeholder id, which `Ingest` already drops silently the same way it drops every other storage error from a connector. This is the "one-off cleanup for existing stores" as a standing, idempotent self-heal on every connect rather than a separate migration: a migration would run once, at whatever version introduced it, and could never catch a placeholder created by a bug fixed after that version shipped; running the same cheap check on every sync needs no version to key off and keeps working for as long as a placeholder can ever be created at all. `resolveSendPeer` applies the same unconditional reasoning for the same reason: a send can be the first thing to touch a peer after a restart, before any sync has run.

A direct chat (`"chat:<id>"`) has no placeholder shape of its own to upgrade from - `placeholderConversation` already gives it the same id a full resolution does - so `placeholderRemoteID` reports nothing for one, and the unconditional check above is a no-op for every such dialog.

## A file picker dialog never runs in the shell process

Picking a file for the composer's attach button, or for "Set notification sound…", crashed the whole Omarchy shell: `QtQuick.Dialogs.FileDialog`'s native dialog on this desktop runs GTK3 in-process (`platformthemes` → `libgtk-3` → `gtk_widget_size_allocate`), and a `g_settings_apply`/`dconf` failure inside that GTK code `SIGABRT`ed the entire shared process - the bar, every notification and every other plugin, not just this one's own window. A crash inside one plugin's dialog must never be able to take every other plugin down with it (see the superseded note above).

The fix moves the dialog itself out of process: `backend/internal/portal` calls `org.freedesktop.portal.FileChooser.OpenFile` over the session bus (the same `github.com/godbus/dbus/v5` dependency `notify` already uses, following its exact pattern - a `Bus` interface with a production `dbusBus` and a `Dial` seam for tests, a sender check on the `Request.Response` signal so no other local process can spoof a chosen file, bounded parsing of that signal's body) and waits for the portal's own `Request.Response` signal. The dialog itself then runs inside `xdg-desktop-portal`'s own process (`-gtk` or `-hyprland` on Omarchy) - a crash there can no longer reach this shell at all. `app.Commands.PickFile` exposes it as a new, additive `files.pick` method (`{purpose, title}` → `{path}`), `purpose` choosing the filters ("attach": images, video and anything else; "sound": the audio formats `audiosafe` can actually validate) - a plain app-level decision kept out of the leaf `portal` package, which knows nothing about attachments or notification sounds.

**Cancelling is not a failure.** `portal.Picker.Pick` returns `(path, ok, err)` rather than using an empty path or a sentinel error for "the user picked nothing": `ok` false with `err` nil means cancelled, so `files.pick`'s result simply omits `path`, the same convention `sounds.fetch` already uses for "nothing playable was found." `err` is reserved for the dialog not being showable at all.

**The fallback, and why it stays local to the UI.** A portal failure (no portal reachable, the `OpenFile` call itself failing) becomes `*app.FilePickError`, carrying only a fixed `Reason` string ("unavailable") across the protocol as the internal error's `data` field - the same shape `*app.MediaFetchError` already uses for `media.fetch`'s own safe reason category, so `server/errors.go` gained one more `errors.As` branch rather than a new pattern. The UI reads it with the existing `Rpc.errorReason(error)` helper and, only then, falls back to a local, non-native `QtQuick.Dialogs.FileDialog` (`options: FileDialog.DontUseNativeDialog`) kept in `Composer.qml` (the attach button) and `ListController.qml` (the sound picker) for exactly this case - a pure-Qt dialog with no GTK involved at all, never the native one that crashed the shell, and never attempted first. Composer, which never calls the service itself (see qml.md), signals `attachRequested()` up to `ComposerController.pickAttachFile()`, which runs the portal round trip and only asks Composer to open its own fallback (`openFallbackPicker()`) once `pickAttachFile` has actually heard back "unavailable"; `ListController` already owns its sound dialog directly, so its own `_setNotificationSound` does the same round trip itself. Either fallback dialog still emits the same `fileAttached(path)` / `notificationSoundPicked(path)` a successful portal pick already resolves to, so nothing downstream (`attachFile`, `media.attach`, `conversations.setNotificationSound`) needed to change.

No test ever opens a real dialog (see ui-testing.md): the Go side fakes `Bus` entirely (`portal`'s own tests) and `FilePicker` (`app`'s and `server`'s); the QML side drives `pickAttachFile`/`_setNotificationSound` against a fake service that answers `files.pick` itself, proving the wiring up to `media.attach`/`conversations.setNotificationSound` without ever reaching the fallback dialog's own `open()` call.

## A scheduled message's own row re-pins the view, rather than being modelled as a real message

A BottomToTop `ListView`'s `header` sits at the visual bottom, right above the composer - where `ScheduledList` (the open conversation's queued "send later" messages) belongs. Growing that header's own height, though, is not something `ListView` repositions the viewport for: unlike a genuine new message landing in `model`, which Qt Quick's own layout correctly keeps pinned to whichever end the user was already viewing, a header item resizing in place left a message queued while already at the newest one scrolled past the clipped viewport - invisible until the conversation was closed and reopened, which lays out fresh and so happens to include the header's current size from the start.

The fix captures `messageList.atYEnd` (for this `verticalLayoutDirection: ListView.BottomToTop` list, "at the end" means showing the newest message, not `atYBeginning`, which tracks the model's own start) the moment `scheduledMessages` is about to change, in `ConversationView._stickToNewest`, before any of the header's own geometry has had a chance to update. The header's `onHeightChanged` then calls `messageList.positionViewAtBeginning()` only when that flag was true: a user scrolled back into older history is never yanked back down just because a message was scheduled, sent now or cancelled elsewhere. Capturing the flag any later - inside the header's own resize handler, for instance - reads a value Qt has already updated to reflect the resize, which is always false by then regardless of where the view actually was beforehand.

A more "correct" fix would fold scheduled rows into the real message model, so Qt's own model-insertion pinning applied for free; that would mean `MessageDelegate` rendering two unrelated row shapes (a message bubble and a clock/send-now/edit/cancel strip) and the composer's own message handling (reactions, retries, delete) growing a null-check for a row kind it can never apply to. Keeping `ScheduledList` a `header:` item, with this one explicit re-pin, is a small, contained fix for a header-specific Qt Quick gap rather than reshaping the whole timeline around it.

## A queued message's own row is keyboard-reachable the same way a highlighted message already is

A report found no prompt anywhere for how to send a queued "send later" message now, edit it or cancel it from the keyboard: the three commands (`scheduled.sendNow`, `.edit`, `.cancel`) already existed, deliberately palette-only like every other "deliberate lookup" command (see `scheduled.show`'s own comment in `ui/lib/Keymap.js`), but nothing on screen said so, and the row itself - `ScheduledList.qml` - took no keyboard input at all.

Giving these three their own keys in the `conversation` context, the way `chat.mute` has `m`, was rejected: there is no single conversation-wide key that unambiguously means "the queued message", and the open chat can hold several of them at once (`ScheduledAction.resolve`'s own `"many"` case), which a bare key press cannot disambiguate the way the palette's picker already does. Instead, `message.highlightNewer`/`highlightOlder` (j/k) - which already move a message id through the open conversation - were extended to run past the newest loaded message into the chat's own queued messages, soonest due first, exactly the way the task that reported this suggested: `MessageHighlight.highlightedScheduledId` names which one, mutually exclusive with `highlightedId` (never both at once, so a message delegate's own highlighted cue and a queued row's own outline are never shown together for the same key press). `moveOlder`/`moveNewer` now take a second argument, `scheduledIds`, computed by `ConversationController._scheduledIds()` from a new `scheduledController` property Panel.qml wires in; reaching the end of either list in either direction clamps, the same way reaching the oldest or newest loaded message already does, and `ConversationController._reconcileHighlightedScheduled()` - run from a `Connections` on `scheduledController`'s own `all` - moves the highlight off a row the moment it stops being queued (sent, cancelled, by this computer or another client), onto whichever is now soonest due, or back to the newest message once none are left, rather than leaving it pointing at a row that no longer exists.

Once a queued row is highlighted, the same three keys a highlighted message already answers to - Enter (`message.open`), `e` (`message.react`) and `d` (`message.delete`) - send it now, edit it and cancel it instead, through a remap `Panel.routeKey` makes *before* the normal action dispatch (`ConversationController.highlightedScheduledId` plus a small `{action: which}` table), calling a new `WindowController.runScheduledActionOn(scheduledId, which)` - the same three outcomes the palette's own `_applyScheduledAction` reaches, skipping its picker since the row is already chosen. `e`, not Shift+E, answers to edit here: there is no "react to a queued message" to free it from, unlike a real message's own `e`/`E` split. `ScheduledList.qml` shows the highlighted row with an accent tint and a thicker border (`Util.alpha(Theme.accent, Style.selectedFillAlpha)`, the same token a selected conversation list row already uses) and its own always-present, opacity-toggled key-hint row (`ScheduledAction.hints`, mirroring `Highlight.hints`' own pattern for a highlighted message), so the hint is visible the moment the row is reached, not only in a tooltip under the mouse.

## Archiving a pinned chat unpins it locally too, only where the service itself requires it

A report found a pinned WhatsApp direct chat archived by a stray keypress, with the list then showing it both "Pinned" and "Archived" at once - a combination the real service never has - and unarchiving it afterward surfaced only the generic "Something went wrong in the helper" banner, with nothing in the helper's own log saying why.

The second half turned out to be the easier bug: `Commands.SetArchived` always stood by its local change even when `organizer.SetArchived` failed, as its own doc comment already said, but the failure itself was handed back unchanged, so `server.rpcError` had nothing it recognised and fell through to the fixed internal error - exactly the categories `pinError` and the media-fetch/file-pick errors already avoid for their own calls. `archiveError` now turns any such failure into `ErrInvalidInput` naming whether it was an archive or an unarchive, and logs the failure's class (today just whether it was a timeout, the one thing this package can tell without reaching into a connector's own errors) to stderr first, through the same `Logger` the retry and media-fetch paths already use - no conversation detail, following privacy.md.

The first half needed to know something `app` has no business knowing on its own: whether archiving a conversation with its service also unpins it there. WhatsApp's own `appstate.BuildArchive` already bundles an unpin mutation into the archive patch whenever `archive` is true - confirmed by reading whatsmeow's own `encode.go` rather than assumed - so a WhatsApp conversation this process asks the server to archive is never both pinned and archived with WhatsApp itself; the bug was purely local, `Commands.SetArchived` never clearing the local pin to match, so a pin set before the service's own correcting event arrived (or that never arrives at all, once that event only follows a successful service call) stayed stuck. Telegram is different on purpose: `MessagesToggleDialogPin` and `FoldersEditPeerFolders` are separate calls with nothing coupling them, and Telegram's own apps keep a chat pinned within the archive folder, so forcing an unpin there would fight the real service rather than agree with it.

`connector.ArchiveUnpinner` (`UnpinsOnArchive() bool`) is the capability WhatsApp's `Connector` reports and Telegram's does not, read through `Manager.ArchiveUnpins(accountID)` the same way every other optional connector capability is - false for an account with no connector running right now, or one that does not implement it. `app.ArchiveUnpinner` mirrors it at the one method `Commands` actually needs. `SetArchived` checks it before doing anything else: when archiving and the account's own service requires it, the local pin clears in the same call that sets `archived`, so the list never shows both, regardless of whether the service call that follows succeeds.

## Four guardrails make the stray archive report hard to repeat, not just fixed once

The report behind the fix above also named the mechanics that let it happen at all: a bare "a" archived a pinned chat with no warning, no second key to confirm it and no way back, while the user was apparently typing somewhere else entirely. Fixing the pin/archive combination and the swallowed error (above) closes that one report; these four close the surrounding gap so the next stray keypress cannot do the same kind of silent damage.

**A modifier for single-key destructive actions.** `chat.archiveRead` moved from a bare `a` to `A` (Shift+A), the same way `message.edit` already answers only to Shift+E rather than a bare `e` - a lone uppercase letter already means Shift in `Keymap.parseSpec`, so this needed no new parsing, only moving the binding. Auditing every other plain-letter binding for the same risk (archives, deletes, blocks, hides) found nothing else to move: `message.delete`'s `d` only ever opens the delete question, never deletes on its own, the same as `chat.block`'s and `chat.reportSpam`'s own palette-only confirmations; `chat.archive` and `chat.hide` have no key of their own at all, reached only through the palette; there is no "leave a group" command yet. `chat.mute` keeps its plain `m`: muting is harmless and, with the undo toast below, reversible with one more key. keys.conf overrides are unaffected either way - they are keyed by action name, never by the shipped default, so a person who had written `chat.archiveRead = a` by hand keeps using `a` regardless of what the default becomes.

**A confirmation before archiving, never before unarchiving.** `ListController.archiveOneOpen` (and `archiveOneTitle`, `_archiveOneId`, `_archiveOneKind`) ask "Archive *title*?" before `chat.archive` (going from unarchived to archived) or `chat.archiveRead` ever reach the service, through a new `ArchiveConfirm.qml`; unarchiving (`chat.archive` the other way) applies at once, since it only reverses something already confirmed, the same asymmetry `chat.unblock` already has against `chat.block`. Rather than adding a second key context and a second accept/cancel action pair, this reuses the existing `archiveConfirm` context and its `archiveAll.accept`/`archiveAll.cancel` bindings (and `ArchiveAllConfirm`'s own bulk question for "Archive all read"): `Navigation.buildState`'s `archiveConfirmOpen` is true while either `archiveAllOpen` or `archiveOneOpen` is (never both at once), and `confirmArchiveAllRead`/`cancelArchiveAllRead` branch on which one it is. This keeps Keymap.js, Actions.js and the Escape chain in Navigation.js untouched for this part, at the cost of those two function names now covering a case broader than "all".

**A generalised undo, not just for archive.** `ui/lib/Undo.js` (`describeToggle`, `describeBlock`) is pure: given the method, field, id, the value just set and a label for each direction, it returns the toast text ("Archived *title* · u to undo") and the exact request that reverses it. `ListController._offerUndo` stores that descriptor as `pendingUndo` and shows the notice; `undo()` (bound to the free key `u`, in both the list and conversation contexts) replays it and clears it, so only the newest action is ever undoable and only for as long as its own toast is showing - `clearNotice` (called wherever the toast dismisses, by its own timeout, a click or Escape) clears `pendingUndo` too, rather than leaving a stale "u" live after its explanation has gone. Archive, mute and hide all now offer it through the one shared `_setOnCurrent`, widened to take the two labels; pin already had its own key-free path and gained the same labels. Block offers it through `confirmBlock` calling `Undo.describeBlock`, reversed through `contacts.unblock` - the exact call `chat.unblock` itself already makes. A spam report and `chat.unblock` itself offer no undo of their own: the former also hides the chat, with nothing simple left to reverse it against, and the latter only ever reverses a block already confirmed once.

**A typing guard for the list and an open conversation, nowhere else - and only for a few actions, not every key.** The first version held back *any* non-navigation action within 300ms of the one before it, which broke real, fast keyboard use: `tests/qml/Flows`'s own `waitEEditCancelledAndBlurred` step failed because Shift+E (open the edit) followed closely by `i` (focus the composer) lost the `i`, so the edit was never cancelled - and the same would have hit anyone chaining Esc→E, E→i or r→i quickly, which is exactly how a proficient keyboard user moves. Confirmed by running the *unmodified* `tests/qml/Flows/shell.qml` against the fixed code and reproducing the same failure at the same step: the production code was at fault, not a flaky test.

`ui/lib/TypingGuard.js`'s `next(action, now, state, navigationActions, guardedActions)` now only ever holds back an action in `GUARDED_ACTIONS` - archive, archive-and-mark-read, mute, pin, hide, block, report spam, delete and mark read/unread: the conversation-changing actions `ui/lib/Undo.js` knows how to reverse, plus mark read/unread, which it does not but which still loses something without warning. Everything else - compose.focus, message.edit, message.reply, message.react, chat.open, escape, any palette or search opener, and navigation - always runs, however fast it follows another key, since none of those loses anything on its own. An *unmatched* key (`action` is `""`, the shape an ordinary typed word takes in a context where none of its letters are bound) is never itself held back either, but still updates the tracked burst state, since a run of such keys followed by a guarded action's own key is the actual "typing in the wrong place" signal this exists to catch - and, before this fix, such a key never even reached the guard at all, since `Panel.routeKey` only called it once a binding had already matched. `NAVIGATION_ACTIONS` (`cursor.*`, `message.highlight*`, `scroll.*`) are exempt both ways, as before: always allowed, and left out of the state entirely, so moving fast and then reaching for any other action right afterwards is never itself mistaken for the start of a burst.

`Panel.routeKey` calls `listController.guardTyping(action)` for every plain, printable key with no Ctrl or Alt held (checked with `/^[\x20-\x7e]$/`, not merely `text.length === 1`: Escape's, Enter's and Tab's own `key.text` is a single *non-printable* control character, not an empty string, which an earlier version of this check missed and which silently ate the second of two Escapes in a row) while the context is `list` or `conversation` - never `compose` - before checking whether `Keymap.match` found anything at all, so an unmatched key still reaches it. A held-back key shows "Press *key* to write a message", reading the live `compose.focus` binding rather than a hard-coded "i".

`tests/qml/KeyboardOverlays`, which deliberately drives several different single-key overlays back to back about 100ms apart (`e`, not itself guarded, then `d`, which is), still needed its own explicit pause between those even under the narrower rule: `e` still extends the tracked burst state, even though it can never itself be held back, so `d` arriving under 300ms later would still be. Two different, deliberate shortcuts a proficient user might really choose that close together are not a mistake, but an artificial, fixed 100ms test cadence is not how a real typing burst looks either - the test says so with a named wait rather than asking the guard to special-case it.

**The typing guard widened again: a mistyped burst must change nothing at all, not just skip the few guarded actions.** A report found typing an ordinary word ("hello") with the conversation list focused opened the selected chat (on the third letter, `l`, one of `chat.open`'s own keys) and then let the rest of the word land on whatever key the newly-open conversation bound it to. `chat.open` and `compose.focus` were never in `GUARDED_ACTIONS` - opening or writing loses nothing on their own - so the narrower rule above never touched them, which is exactly how a stray `l` mid-word reached `chat.open`.

The fix adds a second, independent check to `TypingGuard.next`, on top of the first: once an *unmatched* key - `action === ""`, the shape an ordinary letter takes once it lands on one nothing in the context is bound to - has been seen anywhere earlier in the current burst, every action after it, navigation included, is held back too, for the rest of that burst. One boolean, carried forward and reset the same way `lastAt`/`lastAction` already were, is enough: `h` and `e` in "hello" are both unmatched, so the burst is already flagged by the second key, well before `l` ever reaches `chat.open`.

A numeric alternative was tried first and reverted: "three or more printable keys in a row, matched or not" sounded like it would catch a word built entirely of letters that happen to be bound to something (no unmatched key at all), but it broke `tests/qml/ListController`'s own `checkTypingGuard`, which chains `chat.mute`, `chat.pin` (suppressed by the first check above) and a navigation key, then `chat.mute` again and `compose.focus` - five real, deliberate key presses with nothing unmatched among them, and the count alone flagged the third onward, swallowing the mode switch at the end exactly the way the very first typing guard once swallowed `i` after Shift+E. There is no way to tell "three real shortcuts chained fast" apart from "a three-letter word that happens to parse as three bindings" by timing alone, and an unmatched key is the one signal this guard actually has, so the rule stops there: a word built entirely of letters this context has bound to something is not caught - the same gap the original, narrower design always had - but anything containing a genuine typo (the overwhelming case in practice, since most contexts bind only a handful of single letters) is.

Navigation answers to this same check, not a separate one: a navigation key is held back once an unmatched key has already been seen in the burst - `j` in "jam" must not scroll, since `a` already marked the burst as a typed word by the time `j` (`cursor.down`'s own key) comes around a second time - but is otherwise always allowed, however many of it or whatever real shortcut preceded it: repeated `j`/`k`, `gg`, and - the case the numeric rule broke - navigation right after any number of real actions, guarded or not. `NAVIGATION_ACTIONS` no longer get a structurally different treatment from everything else; they read the exact same tracked flag, just with their own `allow` expression (`!hadUnmatched` rather than also checking `GUARDED_ACTIONS`, which never names a navigation action anyway).

Escape resets both checks: `ListController.resetBurst()` (called from `Panel.routeKey` whenever the matched action is `"escape"`) clears the tracked state outright, so a deliberate Escape then a real shortcut typed quickly afterwards never inherits a burst that started before the Escape, purely because not enough real time happened to pass in between. Escape was never itself tracked (it fails `Panel.routeKey`'s own `isPlainKey` check, its `key.text` being a control character), so without this it could only ever *end* a burst by the clock running out, never on request.

**"Shift+E does nothing" was the same first-version bug, already fixed, with no test ever proving it.** A later report described Shift+E doing nothing on a highlighted own message. Reproducing it with a real `t.keyClick(Qt.Key_E, Qt.ShiftModifier)` against the current code, through `listController.guardTyping` (the real `Panel.routeKey` path - `tests/qml/MessageHighlight`'s own local `routeKey` had never called it at all, so it could not have caught this), found nothing to fix: `message.edit` is not in `GUARDED_ACTIONS` and never was, so it already runs regardless of what preceded it, on its own or right after another quick key (confirmed both ways). Checking out `ui/lib/TypingGuard.js` from the first version above and rerunning the same test reproduced exactly the reported symptom - `message.edit` held back the moment any plain key, matched or not, had landed within 300ms before it - confirming the report described that already-fixed version, most likely from an installed build that predated it; checking it back in restored the pass. `tests/qml/MessageHighlight` keeps both shapes now (`checkShiftEEditsHighlightedMessage`), closing the coverage gap for good: the only existing coverage of Shift+E, in `tests/qml/Flows`, always preceded it with Escape, which never reaches the guard at all, so it could never have caught this.

## WhatsApp app-state patches (pin, archive, mark-read) retry once after a catch-up fetch, and failures classify generically for the app layer

A real account's own log showed every pin, archive and mark-read app-state patch failing: archive and unarchive logged `conversations: set archived failed (archived=true, error_class=other)` (and the same for `false`), pinning failed the same way through the generic internal error, and an earlier mark-read log already showed `chat_patch=failed, chat_patch_error=rejected`. All three send through `dev.sendAppState`, which wraps whatsmeow's `Client.SendAppState`; reading `appstate.go` in the pinned whatsmeow module confirmed all three mutations (`BuildPin`, `BuildArchive`, `BuildMarkChatAsRead`) share one patch type, `appstate.WAPatchRegularLow`, so one account-wide sync problem with that one collection explains every symptom at once, rather than three unrelated bugs.

`Client.sendAppState` already retries once on an explicit 409 conflict, but only by applying the single conflict response's own patches before resending - it never walks every page the server has the way an ordinary background resync (`fetchAppState`, looping on `HasMorePatches`) does. An account whose local copy of `regular_low` fell behind by more than that one response's own page still conflicts on the retry and whatsmeow gives up, returning `ErrAppStateUpdate` - the same error for an outright rejection and an under-caught-up conflict, since the structured error code only ever reaches this connector formatted into that error's own message, never as a separate, comparable value (confirmed by reading `sendAppState`'s own error construction; extracting it would mean comparing error text, which errors.md forbids).

`appstate.go`'s `sendAppStateRecovering(ctx, dev, patch)` is the fix, shared by `organize.go`'s `SetPinned`/`SetArchived` and `markread.go`'s `sendChatReadState`: on `ErrAppStateUpdate`, it calls the new `device.fetchAppState` (a plain, incremental `FetchAppState(name, false, false)`, every page) and retries the patch exactly once more. A rejection that was never a version conflict at all just fails the same way again after the extra round trip - harmless, since this connector cannot tell the two apart up front.

`classifyAppStateErr` joins one of two new, service-agnostic sentinels into the final error - `connector.ErrOrganizeSyncIncomplete` for `appstate.ErrKeyNotFound` (this device's own app-state keys or version never caught up) and `connector.ErrOrganizeRejected` for `ErrAppStateUpdate` itself - so `app.organizeFailureClass` can log a precise `sync`/`rejected`/`timeout`/`other` class without `app` ever importing whatsmeow: the same boundary `connector.ErrPinLimit` already draws. `Commands.pinError` now gets the exact same treatment `archiveError` already had (both call `organizeFailureClass`): any pin service failure beyond the pin limit itself becomes a safe `ErrInvalidInput`, logged first, instead of the generic internal error - `archiveError`'s own fix, previously only for archive.

## Unarchiving restores the pin archiving took away, both locally and with the service

WhatsApp's own archive always unpins a chat there too (see "Archiving a pinned chat unpins it locally too" above), which `Commands.SetArchived` already mirrors locally so the two flags never disagree. That left a chat permanently unpinned the first time it was ever archived: nothing remembered that it had been pinned, so unarchiving it later brought back everything else except the pin - not what a person expects from "archive, then bring back."

`conversations` gained two more columns, `pinned_before_archive` and `pin_order_before_archive` (additive migration, `backend/internal/store/migrate.go`), meaningless and left at 0 for a conversation that was not pinned when it was archived. `Store.RememberPinnedBeforeArchive` copies the current `pinned`/`pin_order` into them, called by `Commands.SetArchived` *before* it clears the local pin to match the service - `SetPinned(ctx, id, false)` right after would otherwise already have zeroed `pin_order` by the time anything tried to remember it. `Store.RestorePinnedFromArchive` does the reverse on unarchive: it restores `pinned`/`pin_order` from those two columns when they say the chat was pinned, clears both either way (so a second archive/unarchive cycle with nothing pinned this time cannot replay a stale, older value), and reports whether it actually restored anything.

That report is what lets `Commands.SetArchived` also ask the service to re-pin: once its own `SetArchived(ctx, conv, false)` call succeeds, a restored pin is given back there too (`rePinAfterUnarchive`, calling the same `Organizer.SetPinned` `Commands.SetPinned` already uses), matching WhatsApp's own app, which does not re-pin a chat on unarchive by itself. This is best effort and logged only, the same as `MarkRead`'s own chat-read mutation: the local pin already stands regardless, and a later resync corrects any miss, so a re-pin failure is never folded into the unarchive's own result or returned to the caller. Nothing is attempted when the unarchive's own service call failed - there is nothing there yet to re-pin.

## A legacy thumbnail is re-sanitized on every read, not trusted once a migration has run

A security review found that upgrading past the commit that added `imagesafe.Sanitize` for message thumbnails (stickers, photo and video previews, link previews) did nothing for a thumbnail already on disk: `scanMessage` decoded whatever a previous version had stored and handed it straight to the UI, so a malicious thumbnail received and stored before upgrading could still reach the compressed-metadata exhaustion path the sanitizer exists to close, offline, with no new message involved at all.

**The migration.** `migrationStep` (`backend/internal/store/migrate.go`) now holds either a plain SQL string or a Go `repair` function, so a change that needs real code - deciding whether a stored thumbnail still passes `imagesafe.Sanitize`, something no SQL statement can do - can still run as an ordinary, versioned step through the same `PRAGMA user_version` counter and one-transaction-per-step machinery every earlier migration already uses. The new step, `sanitizeLegacyThumbnails` (`backend/internal/store/thumbsafe.go`), finds every message whose stored `media` JSON mentions a `thumb` field at all (a cheap `LIKE` prefilter before any JSON parsing), re-sanitizes it through `imagesafe.Thumbnail` - the same bound a connector already applies to one it has just received - and writes back only the rows that actually changed: a thumbnail that fails outright is cleared to `""`, never left as it was or silently dropped from the row entirely, and one that merely carried something the sanitizer strips is kept, re-encoded. It is bounded by `imagesafe.Thumbnail`'s own per-image decode limits and idempotent, since sanitizing an already-sanitized thumbnail a second time yields the same bytes.

**Why the read path checks again, forever, rather than trusting the migration once it has run.** The migration fixes every row already on disk, but it is a one-time pass: it says nothing about a thumbnail a *future* write path stores after it has already run, if that path ever forgets to sanitize first - exactly the class of mistake that created this finding in the first place. Rather than ask every one of `app`'s many call sites that read a stored message to remember to re-check it (a dozen or more, in `React`, `Vote`, `Retry`, `Send`'s own re-read after dispatch, `Inject`, and others - fragile precisely because remembering at each call site is what failed before), `scanMessage` itself - the one function every stored message passes through, in `store`, whichever of `Message`, `Messages`, `MessageByRemote`, `UpdateMessageStatus` or `EditMessage` reached it - now runs every thumbnail through the same `sanitizedThumb` helper on every read, not only the first one after an upgrade. `imagesafe.Sanitize`'s own cost profile makes this cheap enough to run unconditionally: every format's sanitizer bounds itself to a chunk or segment walk plus a header-only `image.DecodeConfig` confirmation, never a full pixel decode, so paging through even a long conversation costs a small, fixed amount of work per thumbnail, not a decode that scales with image size. This is a stronger guarantee than checking only in `app` would have been: it also covers `store`'s own future call sites, not just the ones `app` happens to wrap today, and it needs no persisted "already checked" marker or in-memory cache to stay cheap, since there is nothing expensive enough here to need memoizing.

**What this means for the UI side.** Every `Image` that ever sources a thumbnail as a `data:` URI (`StickerView.qml`, `PhotoView.qml`, `PhotoViewer.qml`, `LinkPreview.qml`, `InlineVideo.qml`) already sets `sourceSize` to its own fixed display box, which bounds what Qt allocates for the final bitmap regardless of what a thumbnail declares; this was unaffected by the finding, which was about Qt's decoder inflating compressed ancillary chunks *during* decode, before `sourceSize` ever comes into play, so no QML change was needed.

## A legacy full image, sticker or GIF file is swept once per on-disk format version, not re-sanitized on every serve

The thumbnail finding above was about one column in the database; the same class of gap existed for the actual media files `cache.Cache` and `cache.Outgoing` keep on disk. Auditing every file-producing path in `backend/` found:

- `cache.Cache.Fetch` and `FetchNamed` (the downloaded media cache, `~/…/media/`): a photo, sticker or GIF is sanitized through `imagesafe` only the moment `connector.SaveImageMedia` writes it on a cache *miss* (`fetchImage` in each connector); `Fetch`'s own cache-hit path (`os.Stat` succeeds, return the path) never looks at the file again. A file a pre-`imagesafe` release wrote is served to the UI's `Image`/`AnimatedImage` exactly as it was, forever, after an upgrade.
- `cache.Outgoing.Store` (the outgoing attachment area): a user-attached or pasted photo is sanitized by `app.describeAttachment`/`sanitizeAttachedPhoto` before the message is first sent; `app.localAttachment`, which `FetchMedia` checks before ever asking a connector to download anything, returns an existing outgoing copy's path with no check at all. An attachment copied in before that sanitizing existed (`6dfef07` and earlier) is served the same unchecked way.
- A video, voice note, audio message or plain file in either area needed no equivalent fix: `app.DecodeAsForMedia` (`connector.SanitizeIfGIF`), `VideoPlayableForMedia` (`connector.VideoPlayable`/`videosafe`), `AudioPlayableForMedia` (`connector.AudioPlayable`/`audiosafe`) and `app.MediaRisk` are all called by `server.mediaFetch` on *every* `media.fetch`, cache hit or not, regardless of how old the file is - the file's own bytes are what each of these reads, never a decision cached from whenever it first landed on disk. A photo or sticker had no such per-fetch check at all: once `FetchMedia` returns a path, the UI hands it straight to `Image`/`AnimatedImage`, the one place a sender's bytes reach a Qt decoder with nothing of this helper's own in between.
- Custom notification sounds (`cache.Sounds`) also needed nothing further: `app.playableSoundPath` already calls `audiosafe.Validate` on every play, not only when the file was first stored.
- Avatars and the sign-in QR code do not exist as a gap: this plugin has no avatar feature, and the QR PNG `connector`'s own sign-in flow shows is generated by this process itself (`rsc.io/qr`), never bytes a sender or service controls.

**The fix: a format-version marker per area, checked once at startup, gating the server.** `cache.Cache` and `cache.Outgoing` each gained `FormatVersion`/`SetFormatVersion` (a plain integer in a `.media-format` file next to the area's own files) and `Revalidate(check RevalidateCheck)`, which walks every finished file in the directory (skipping a `.part` fill in progress and the marker itself) and removes whatever `check` reports `keep=false` for. Both stay `cache`-package-only (no `imagesafe` import: `cache`'s own depguard rule allows only `$gostd`), taking the actual safety decision as a plain injected function - the same dependency-inversion `cache.Outgoing` already uses for `MessageExists`.

`connector.RevalidateDownloadedImage` and `RevalidateOutgoingImage` are that function for each area, living in `connector` (which already depends on `imagesafe`). Both peek a file's first bytes (`imagesafe.LooksLikeImage`, an additive, structural-check-free export) before touching anything else: a file that is not one of the four formats `imagesafe` recognizes - a video, voice note or plain document, every one of which already gets its own kind-specific check on every single fetch (see above) - is left completely untouched and reported `keep=true`, the same "never trust a filename or kind, only the bytes" rule `SanitizeIfGIF` already set. One that does look like an image is re-run through `imagesafe.Sanitize`: `RevalidateDownloadedImage` picks `AnimatedGIF` for a real GIF file's own magic bytes and `FullImage` otherwise, the same choice `fetchImage` itself makes for a fresh download; `RevalidateOutgoingImage` always runs `SanitizeOutgoingImageFile`, the one profile every outgoing attachment is sanitized through today, regardless of whether it happens to be a GIF. A file that still sanitizes cleanly is rewritten in place only when the result actually differs (no needless write for a file that was already fine); one that fails outright is reported `keep=false`, and `Revalidate` removes it.

`backend/medialegacy.go`'s `revalidateLegacyMedia`, called from `serve` right after `newMediaCaches` and *before* `wire` and `srv.Start`, runs this for both areas whenever an area's own `FormatVersion` is older than `legacyMediaFormatVersion` (or unreadable), then records the current version - even when the sweep itself hit a problem with one file, so one bad file can never leave every other file, or the marker itself, stuck being re-checked on every single start forever. Because this runs to completion before `srv.Start` ever lets a `media.fetch` request reach `app.Commands`, there is no window where a stale cache hit could be served mid-startup: the "defense in depth on the serving path" this fix also needed falls out of the ordering for free, rather than needing a second, separate per-request check layered on top of what `DecodeAsForMedia`/`VideoPlayableForMedia`/`AudioPlayableForMedia`/`MediaRisk` already give every other kind.

**Why a format-version marker and a bounded sweep, not re-sanitizing a full image on every single serve the way a thumbnail's read path does.** A thumbnail is capped at 2 MB and `imagesafe.Thumbnail`'s own decode limits, read from the database on every message list scroll regardless; re-sanitizing it every time was already free. A full photo or GIF is up to 32 MB (`imagesafe.FullImage`/`AnimatedGIF`'s `MaxInputLen`) and today never has its bytes read by this helper at all on a cache hit - `Fetch` only `os.Stat`s the path and hands it back. Reading and re-sanitizing the whole file on every `media.fetch`, including the common case of reopening a conversation and scrolling past photos already sent weeks ago, would add real, size-scaling I/O and CPU to a path that is supposed to be instant once something is cached, for a check that is only ever needed once, right after an upgrade. A one-time, versioned sweep gives the exact same guarantee - nothing already on disk escapes today's `imagesafe` checks - at a cost that is paid once per format change, not once per view, which `resources.md` and the thumbnail fix's own reasoning both point toward once the two areas' very different read patterns are taken into account.

**Why removal, not always trying to recover a rejected file.** `cache.Cache` already redownloads on any cache miss, so removing an image that still fails `Sanitize` is exactly as safe as it dropping out of the LRU eviction `prune` already does - the next `FetchMedia` call simply refetches it from the service, confirmed by `fetchIntoCache`'s own fallback to `Fetch`/`FetchNamed`'s `fill` closure. For the outgoing area, removing a rejected attachment's copy is what `app.restoreAttachment` already handles for an outgoing copy gone missing for any reason: it re-copies (and re-sanitizes) from the original file on disk, when `media.OriginalPath` still points at one, or reports the existing, safe `errAttachmentUnavailable` ("attach it again") when it does not - the one case, a clipboard paste with no original to go back to, this sweep cannot silently repair, and the existing fallback already treats that failure as ordinary, not a crash.

**This sweep-only approach turned out not to be enough; see the next entry for why and what replaced it.**

## Every photo or sticker `media.fetch` ever returns is re-checked against `imagesafe` on that same call, not only once after an upgrade

The format-version sweep above assumed a file's own *bytes* never needed checking again once the sweep had run, or once a connector had sanitized it the moment it was written. Three follow-up security reviews, run against the commit that added the sweep, each found a case that assumption missed, all with the same shape: something other than a fresh, sanitized download can still end up at the exact path a photo or sticker fetch serves straight to a QML `Image`.

- **The sweep's own "doesn't look like an image, leave it alone" rule was the gap, not a safeguard.** `RevalidateDownloadedImage` kept, untouched, any file whose first bytes were not PNG/JPEG/GIF/WebP, reasoning that such a file "was never going to reach Qt's own image decoder in the first place." That holds for a video, voice note or file, each already checked on every fetch regardless of age - but not for a legacy photo cached as `<id>.jpg` or a sticker cached as `<id>.<sender's extension>` whose actual bytes are SVG, TIFF, BMP, PDF or any other format a Qt image plugin decodes by sniffing content, never a cache file's own name. The sweep reported `keep=true` for exactly those files, so they were trusted forever after the upgrade that was supposed to close this.
- **An edit that changes a message's media kind reused whatever the old kind had already cached.** `fetchIntoCache` named every cache slot after the message's id alone. A voice note, file or audio message - none of them image-checked - cached its bytes under `<id>.<sniffed extension>`; if the sender then edited that same message into a photo or sticker (WhatsApp's edit is end-to-end encrypted, so any kind change is possible with no new click from the user), the kind-specific fixed name a photo fetch uses, or the generic `FetchNamed` lookup a sticker fetch uses, could land on that exact same id and serve the old, never-image-checked file as the new kind.
- **`recopyFromOriginal` (a failed send's retry) never sanitized what it recopied.** `storeAttachment`, the one function it calls to put a file back in the outgoing area, never ran `sanitizeAttachedPhoto` the way `describeAttachment` already does for every other path into that area; an attachment pending from a release older than that check, once its outgoing copy went missing, came back raw on the next retry.
- Two smaller bugs compounded the sweep's own blind spots: a file the sweep could not even read was reported `keep=false` with an error, but `cache.Revalidate`'s own entry point returned early on that error without ever calling `removeIfPresent`, so an unreadable file was left exactly where it was; and the format marker was written unconditionally, even when revalidating one file had failed, so a problem the sweep could not resolve was still recorded as fully checked.

**The fix: one guarantee at the point every photo or sticker path is actually handed to the UI, replacing more ingest-side patching.** `app.Commands.FetchMedia` - the one function `server.mediaFetch`, `SaveMedia` and `CopyMedia` all go through before a path ever reaches the protocol - now calls `servedPath` on every path it is about to return, whatever produced it: a cache hit, a fresh download, or an outgoing message's own local copy. For a message whose kind the UI decodes as a still image (`domain.MediaPhoto`, `domain.MediaSticker`; an animated "GIF" video already has the equivalent guarantee through `DecodeAsForMedia`'s own call to `connector.SanitizeIfGIF`, unchanged here), `servedPath` runs `connector.EnsureImageSafe` - a new export mirroring `SanitizeIfGIF`'s own shape - against the profile the kind picks (`imagesafe.FullImage` for a photo, `imagesafe.Thumbnail` for a sticker, since a sticker is always drawn small). `EnsureImageSafe` re-reads and re-sanitizes the file regardless of its own history: nothing about *why* a file is at that path - the sweep's own heuristic, an edit that changed kind, an unsanitized recopy, or genuinely nothing at all - changes what gets checked or how. A file that is not one of the four formats `imagesafe.Sanitize` recognizes, fails its own checks outright, or cannot even be read is removed on the spot, never served. For a download, `recoverUnsafeImage` gets exactly one fresh retry from the service - a connector sanitizes a photo or sticker itself the instant it writes one, so a legitimate retry is expected to come back clean - before failing safely as the same kind of `*MediaFetchError` any other failed fetch already produces; an outgoing message's own local copy, with nowhere else to come from, fails safely on the first rejection.

**Why this replaces the sweep's own reasoning rather than sitting beside it.** The previous entry's "why not re-sanitize on every serve" argument rested on the sweep already closing every gap a cache hit could have; once that turned out to be false in three different ways, the cost argument needed re-checking against what `imagesafe.Sanitize` actually costs, not just against how often `FetchMedia` is called. Every format's sanitizer bounds itself to a header or chunk walk plus a header-only dimension check, the same "cheap enough to run unconditionally" profile the thumbnail fix (above) already established for the database path - confirmed here by the same `imagesafe.Profile.MaxInputLen` caps a connector's own fresh download already enforces before ever reading a file into memory. Running it again on an already-sanitized file costs that same bounded walk and nothing more: `Sanitize` is idempotent, so a file a connector just wrote is confirmed unchanged (`bytes.Equal`), never rewritten a second time. The sweep itself is left in place - a bad legacy file removed before the helper ever starts serving requests is one less unnecessary network round trip the very next fetch would otherwise need - but it is no longer the thing anything relies on for safety; `servedPath`'s own guarantee holds whether or not the sweep ever ran, or ever could have covered a case like an edit changing kind, which has nothing to do with a file's age at all.

**Cache identity now includes the media kind, so an edit never reuses a different kind's file.** `cacheIdentity` (`app/media.go`) names a message's cache slot by its id *and* its current media kind, not the id alone; `fixedMediaName`, `fetchIntoCache`'s `FetchNamed` branch and `outgoingCacheName` all build their names from it. A voice note cached as `<id>.voice.<ext>` and that same message edited into a photo now looks for `<id>.photo.jpg` - a clean cache miss, never the voice note's own file - so the kind-change gap above is closed structurally, not just by `servedPath` happening to catch it after the fact (though it still would). `Ingest.Edited` does not go further and delete the old kind's now-orphaned file: it simply ages out of `cache.Cache`'s own LRU `prune` like any other file nothing has asked for recently, which needed no new code to be true.

**The sweep's two smaller bugs are fixed too, independent of the serving-side guarantee.** `RevalidateDownloadedImage` and `RevalidateOutgoingImage` now remove a file on a read failure (`errors.Join(err, removeIfPresent(path))`) rather than reporting the error and leaving the file exactly as it was; `cache`'s own `revalidateEntry` removes whatever a check reports anything but a clean `keep=true, nil` for, so a bug in a future check implementation that forgets to remove on its own error still cannot leave a file trusted. `medialegacy.go`'s `revalidateArea` now records an area's format marker only once `Revalidate` returns with no error at all - a run that could only resolve a problem file by removing it is still, by this stricter rule, not "fully checked clean," so the area is simply offered to the sweep again next start rather than ever being marked current on the strength of an error it happened to recover from.

**Outgoing recopy now goes through the same sanitizer a fresh attachment gets.** `recopyFromOriginal` calls `sanitizeAttachedPhoto` on whatever `storeAttachment` just copied back, for a photo, exactly like `describeAttachment` already does for a first attach; a recopy that fails this is refused with the existing, safe `errAttachmentUnavailable`, never resent raw. This is belt-and-suspenders with `servedPath`'s own guarantee - even if a future change reintroduced an unsanitized recopy path, `FetchMedia` would still never hand that file to the UI - but fixing it here too means a failed send's retry reports its own refusal immediately, rather than silently storing an unsafe copy that only gets caught the next time something tries to view it.

## Pin and archive patches were still being rejected after the catch-up-and-retry fix - because they, unlike mark-read, were never addressed by LID

The catch-up-and-retry fix above ("WhatsApp app-state patches ... retry once after a catch-up fetch") did not help a real account whose log kept showing `conversations: set archived failed (archived=true, error_class=rejected)` and the matching `set pinned failed` line for every single pin, unpin, archive and unarchive, with no exceptions - a shape that itself pointed away from the catch-up fix's own target (a local copy temporarily behind by more than one page), since that would be expected to recover at least sometimes, not fail identically every time forever.

**Diagnosis.** `sendAppStateRecovering`'s own rejection was still logged with nothing more than `error_class=rejected`: whatsmeow's `SendAppState` formats the server's reply into `ErrAppStateUpdate`'s own message as the XML of its `<error>` node (`fmt.Errorf("%w (%s): %s", ErrAppStateUpdate, patch.Type, &errorTag)`), carrying a numeric `code` and a `text` attribute that never reached this connector's own logs at all. `appStateRejectionDetail` (`appstate.go`) now extracts exactly those two named attributes with a pair of regexps anchored on the attribute's own name (`<error\b[^>]*\bcode="(\d+)"` and the same for `text`), never anything else the node might carry - this is the one place in the package a regexp over an error's own text is used at all, and only for logging, never to decide what `sendAppStateRecovering` does next, which stays entirely on `errors.Is` against `whatsmeow.ErrAppStateUpdate` and the `appstate` package's own sentinels (see errors.md). `logAppStateRejected` writes `server_code`/`server_text` alongside the existing `error_class`, and `logAppStateFetchFailed` now reports the incremental catch-up's own fetch error by class too (`fetchAppStateErrClass`), previously swallowed down to nothing at all.

**The actual root cause, once a real account's server code and text were visible in the log: the same LID-addressing bug an earlier fix already found and fixed for mark-read, but never for pin or archive.** "A chat migrated to LID addressing needs its 'mark chat as read' patch sent by LID, not its phone JID" (above) found that `appstate.BuildMarkChatAsRead`'s mutation index is built from whichever JID it is given, and that WhatsApp's server rejects it outright once an account has migrated to LID addressing and the patch still names the chat by its now-superseded phone JID - fixed there with `lidFormOf`, resolving to the chat's LID when whatsmeow's own LID store has learned one. Reading `appstate/encode.go` in the pinned whatsmeow module confirms `BuildPin` and `BuildArchive` build their own mutation index exactly the same way, `[]string{IndexPin, target.String()}` and `[]string{IndexArchive, target.String()}` - the identical mechanism, on the identical patch type (`WAPatchRegularLow`), with the identical failure mode. `organize.go`'s `SetPinned` and `SetArchived` were simply never given the equivalent `lidFormOf` call `sendChatReadState` already had, so every pin and archive request kept addressing a migrated chat by its phone JID and kept being rejected, catch-up or not - a wrong-address patch fails the same way no matter how many times its own version catches up first, which is exactly why the earlier catch-up fix alone could never have helped this account.

**The fix.** `SetPinned` and `SetArchived` now resolve `target := lidFormOf(ctx, dev, jid)` before building their patch, the same call `sendChatReadState` already makes, and build `appstate.BuildPin`/`BuildArchive` from `target` rather than the chat's original `jid`. Because whatsmeow's chat settings store (`store.ChatSettingsStore`) is keyed by exactly whichever address a mutation's own index named - confirmed by `dispatchAppState` parsing `jid` straight from `mutation.Index[1]` before calling `PutPinned`/`PutArchived` - `reportOrganized`'s own read-back and `pinnedCount`'s per-chat check are both resolved through the same `lidFormOf` call, or a migrated chat's just-applied pin or archive would be sent correctly but then read back from the wrong key and reported as unset. `deleteForMe` (`delete.go`), which builds its own `appstate.IndexDeleteMessageForMe` mutation from the chat JID the same way, is addressed by `lidFormOf` too, and now also goes through `sendAppStateRecovering` rather than a bare `dev.sendAppState`, so a delete-for-me patch gets the same catch-up-and-retry (and, below, full-sync) recovery pin, archive and mark-read already had - nothing in the original report showed delete-for-me failing, but it shares the identical mechanism and there was no reason to leave it as the one app-state patch with neither fix.

**A second round of recovery, for the case the LID fix does not cover: a genuinely corrupted local copy.** Even with the right address, an account's local copy of `regular_low` can still be corrupted or diverged in a way no amount of incremental catching-up can repair - confirmed by reading `appstate/hash.go` and `appstate/decode.go`: `DecodePatches` returns `ErrMismatchingLTHash` when the running hash it keeps no longer agrees with the version number alongside it, and `ErrMismatchingPatchMAC`/`ErrMismatchingContentMAC`/`ErrMismatchingIndexMAC`/`ErrMissingPreviousSetValueOperation` when one mutation's own authentication fails, each one unrecoverable by an incremental fetch since every incremental fetch starts from that same bad local copy. `sendAppStateRecovering` now works through two rounds rather than one: the existing incremental catch-up and retry, and, only if that still leaves a rejection, a full resync from the server's own snapshot (`device.fetchAppState` gained a `fullSync bool` parameter, `FetchAppState(ctx, name, fullSync, false)` - `fullSync` true deletes this device's own stored version for the patch type first, so the next fetch has nothing local left to agree or disagree with and rebuilds both the version and the hash from the server's snapshot alone) and one final retry. This always runs, rather than only when a corrupt-state sentinel is actually seen: this connector already accepted an unconditional extra round trip for the first catch-up on exactly this reasoning ("a rejection that was never a version conflict at all just fails the same way again ... no worse off for the extra round trip"), and the same reasoning extends cleanly to a second round rather than adding a branch that guesses, from a swallowed-until-now fetch error, whether a full sync is worth trying.

## An account whose `regular_low` can never be verified goes local-only, quietly, rather than retrying the full recovery on every action

The two-round recovery above still was not enough for one real account: its own log showed `app state fetch failed (type=regular_low, full_sync=false, error_class=corrupt)`, then the same with `full_sync=true`, then `app state patch rejected (type=regular_low, error_class=rejected, server_code=409, server_text=conflict)` - for every pin, archive and mark-read, forever. This is the open upstream bug [whatsmeow#1301](https://github.com/tulir/whatsmeow/issues/1301): on an account whose phone has moved its chats to LID addressing, WhatsApp's own `regular_low` snapshot fails whatsmeow's LTHash verification against the server, so a full resync rebuilds the exact same unverifiable copy every time, and every `regular_low` write gets rejected with a 409 regardless of LID addressing, catch-up fetches, or how many times either is retried. No whatsmeow release fixes this as of this writing.

**Why not relax verification instead.** The tempting shortcut - accept the server's patches without checking the LTHash or a mutation's own MAC - was rejected outright. That verification is what confirms a patch actually came from WhatsApp's own server and has not been tampered with or dropped in transit; weakening it for `regular_low` would make this connector trust unauthenticated pin, archive and read-state data for every account, not just the one caught by this bug, in exchange for a feature (syncing those three things) this plugin can live without syncing. `docs/decisions.md`'s and `errors.md`'s own rule against ever comparing an error's text to decide behaviour exists for the same reason: correctness here is worth more than convenience.

**The fix: once this is detected, give up on syncing that patch type, quietly, until it might have cleared.** `appstate.go`'s `sendAppStateRecovering` already knows, from `fetchAppStateErrClass`, whether each catch-up round failed because the local copy is corrupt rather than merely behind; when *both* rounds come back that way and the retried patch is still rejected, that is no longer "try again next time" - it is "this account's own copy of this collection can never be reconciled right now." `sendAppStateRecovering` wraps that specific shape in a new, private `errAppStateUnverifiable`, and `classifyAppStateErr` turns it into a new, service-agnostic sentinel, `connector.ErrOrganizeLocalOnly` (deliberately not named after WhatsApp or `regular_low`: the shape - a change that stands locally but can never be told to the service right now - is not inherently WhatsApp-specific, even though no other connector reports it today).

`sendOrganizePatch` (`appstate.go`) is the one choke point `SetPinned`, `SetArchived`, `sendChatReadState` and `deleteForMe` all go through now, replacing their own separate calls to `sendAppStateRecovering`: it checks `Connector.organizeLocalOnly(patch.Type)` first and, once true, returns `connector.ErrOrganizeLocalOnly` at once, with no call to WhatsApp's server at all - the "stop retrying the expensive recovery on every action" half of the fix. The flag is tracked **per app-state patch type**, not as one account-wide switch: pin, archive and mark-read all share `WAPatchRegularLow`, but delete-for-me builds its own patch under `WAPatchRegularHigh` (confirmed by reading `appstate/encode.go`; `delete.go`'s `buildDeleteForMe` sets `appstate.WAPatchRegularHigh` directly, since whatsmeow exposes no `BuildDeleteForMe` helper for this). The real account's own logs only ever showed `regular_low` failing this way; tracking the flag per patch type means delete-for-me keeps working normally unless its own collection is independently found to fail the same way, rather than being switched off by a problem that, so far, is `regular_low`'s alone.

**Persistence and recovery.** `organizehealthstore.go` adds one small table, `organize_health`, to the same per-account `mediaStore` database `chatalias.go` and `keys.go` already use, so a flagged patch type survives a helper restart instead of needing to rediscover the same corrupt-and-rejected failure (a full resync, twice) from scratch the first time something is pinned, archived or deleted again. `Connector.Run` loads `regular_low`'s own saved state right after opening the media store, and a background goroutine (`runOrganizeHealthCheck`, `organizehealth.go`) retries one incremental fetch of `regular_low` once right away and then once a day, for as long as the account is connected, clearing the flag - and logging that it did - the moment that fetch succeeds: a whatsmeow upgrade that fixes this bug, or WhatsApp repairing the account's own server-side state, is noticed without the user ever having to restart the helper. That goroutine runs on a context `waitForStop` derives from `Run`'s own and cancels the instant `Run` returns for any reason, so it can never touch `dev` after `Run`'s deferred `disconnect`/`close` have already torn it down (see concurrency.md).

**The app layer: a notice, never a failure.** `connector.ErrOrganizeLocalOnly` reaching `Commands.SetPinned`, `SetArchived` or `DeleteMessages` is not treated as a service failure at all, unlike every other connector error those three already turn into a safe `ErrInvalidInput`: the local change (already applied to the store before the connector is even asked, for pin and archive; applied regardless of the connector's answer, for delete) stands, and `organizeLocalOnlyHandled` (`organizenotice.go`) publishes a new event, `account.notice` (`{accountId, message}`, see `docs/api.md`), the first time this helper run sees the condition for that account - never on every later pin, archive or delete that finds it still standing. A helper run, not a persisted dismissal, was chosen for that "once" boundary: the underlying condition is already logged once by the connector and resumed from its own persisted flag across a restart, so showing this quiet toast again after a restart is a reasonable, low-noise reminder rather than something that needs its own stored UI state and migration. Mark-read is deliberately left out of this notice path: its chat-read app-state patch already failed silently, logged only, before this change (`sendChatReadState`'s own result is never returned as an error from `MarkRead`), so there was no existing banner there to replace, and its per-message read receipts - a different, non-`regular_low` mechanism - keep clearing the badge and updating WhatsApp's server-side progress exactly as before regardless of this flag.

The UI shows `account.notice` in the existing confirmation toast (`ui/components/Toast.qml`), not the error banner, through a new `Service.accountNotice` property (`ui/service/AppState.qml`) that `WindowController.toastText` falls back to behind whatever a conversation or list action is already confirming; `dismissToast` clears it the same way it clears every other notice, so it never reappears on its own once shown.

**Not changed: the per-message read receipts, and whether this account's chats have actually migrated to LID addressing.** `markReadFrom`'s receipts were already confirmed unaffected by the original LID bug (see the entry above) and nothing here touches them. Whether this particular real account's chats have in fact migrated to LID addressing was not independently confirmed beyond the symptom matching exactly - every pin, unpin, archive and unarchive rejected, with mark-read's own identical bug already proven and fixed the same way on the same account's own earlier report - rather than, say, inspecting that account's own whatsmeow database; `logAppStateRejected`'s new `server_code`/`server_text` fields are what let a future report confirm or rule this out directly instead of guessing from the shape of the failure again.

## A sticker's own emoji fallback, pasted spell-check text, a search snippet and a media-swapping edit were each capable of a shell denial of service

A pre-release security review found four sender- or clipboard-controlled values with no length cap of their own, each reaching omarchy-shell's UI thread uncapped:

- **A sticker's `Media.Emoji` fallback** (WhatsApp's `StickerMessage.Emojis`, Telegram's `DocumentAttributeSticker.Alt`) had no case in `domain.CapMedia` at all, unlike every other sender-controlled media field. `MaxStickerEmojiLength` (32 UTF-16 units, the same reasoning as `MaxReactionEmojiLength`) is the new cap, applied in `CapMedia` at ingest and, like `sanitizedThumb`, re-applied on every read in `scanMessage` (`store.CapMedia` call right after the thumbnail re-check) so a sticker an older release already stored is repaired without a migration - a plain length check costs nothing like an image decode does, so there is no reason to limit the repair to a one-time sweep the way the full-image legacy sweep is. `StickerView.qml`'s emoji fallback `Text` also gained `wrapMode: Text.Wrap`, `maximumLineCount: 2` and `elide: Text.ElideRight` as defence in depth, the same layered approach `ReactionChips.qml` already has for an oversized reaction chip.
- **Spell-checking a pasted clipboard string**: `ComposerController.qml` sent the composer's whole text to `spell.check` uncapped, and `spell.Checker.Check` returned every misspelling with no limit, so Composer.qml's own overlay rebuilt a underline rectangle for each one, with a quadratic `concat` call, on every keystroke and caret move. Fixed at every layer: `Commands.CheckSpelling` now caps the text it scans to `domain.MaxTextLength` (the same cap `messages.send` enforces on outgoing text) and the result to the new `app.MaxSpellMisspellings` (200, documented in `docs/api.md` as an additive, non-breaking limit), Composer.qml's `_recomputeOverlay` builds its rectangle list with `push` instead of repeated `concat` and caps the misspellings it renders to `SpellCheck.MAX_RENDERED_MISSPELLINGS` (200, kept in step with the Go constant the same way `Format.js`'s own mirrored caps are), and the composer's own `TextArea` now caps pasted text to `SpellCheck.MAX_COMPOSER_TEXT_LENGTH` (4096 code points, matching `domain.MaxTextLength`) the moment it lands, since a paste has no length limit of its own the way typing naturally does.
- **The command palette's "Messages" search snippet**: `store.conversationsByTitleOrMessage` returned a matched message's entire `text` as `Match`, up to `domain.MaxIncomingTextLength` (64 KiB), with no window around the match the way a real search result needs. It now calls FTS5's own `snippet()` aux function (`matchSnippetTokens`, 32 tokens) instead of selecting the column directly, the same kind of bounded-window-around-a-hit `bm25()` already proved FTS5's aux functions work correctly here; `Palette.js`'s `messageRows` also runs the result through `Format.previewText` (the same cap the conversation list's own preview already gets) as defence in depth.
- **An edit that swaps a message's media for a different kind, or different bytes of the same kind** (a voice note edited into a photo, say - WhatsApp and Telegram edits are end-to-end encrypted and can change `Media.Kind` entirely): `MessageTimeline.qml`'s `upsert` kept a row's old `mediaPath` and every verdict (`mediaDecodeAs`, `mediaPlayable`, `mediaRisky`, `mediaVerdictsKnown`, `mediaFailed`) across any update, including this one, because the backend's own safety checks are keyed by the message's id, not by its media's own identity. `Timeline.mediaIdentityChanged` (new, alongside the existing `Timeline.mediaJustRetired`) compares `media.kind` and a small set of identifying fields (`fileName`, `size`, `width`, `height`, `duration`, `thumb`, `url`) between the loaded row and the incoming update; `upsert` now drops the cached path and every verdict when either that or `mediaJustRetired` is true, so the new media is always fetched and checked afresh rather than shown through stale verdicts that described different bytes.

## `videosafe` was bounding a video by its container's own dimensions, while FFmpeg decodes at whatever size the video's compressed bitstream declares

A pre-release differential security review found that `videosafe.Validate` read only an MP4/MOV sample entry's own width/height fields, or Matroska's `PixelWidth`/`PixelHeight`, and never the codec configuration the in-process FFmpeg behind QML's `MediaPlayer` (`VideoRuntime.qml`, through `InlineVideo.qml` and `FullVideoPlayer.qml`) actually decodes from. A sender fully controls both independently: a container can declare a small, "safe-looking" width and height while the stream it wraps declares something far larger, and `media.fetch` would report the file playable on the container's word alone. A flat keyframe at an extreme declared size compresses to a few kilobytes but decodes to frames of hundreds of megabytes each - a shell-crashing denial of service the moment the video is opened inline, the normal way to watch one.

**The fix binds what the decoder actually uses, not what the container claims, and never trusts the two to agree without checking.** For H.264 (`avc1`), the only MP4/MOV video codec this package still accepts, `h264sps.go` reads the sample entry's `avcC` box - the exact configuration FFmpeg's own H.264 decoder reads - and parses its single sequence parameter set (SPS) with a small Exp-Golomb bit reader: `pic_width_in_mbs_minus1`, `pic_height_in_map_units_minus1`, `frame_mbs_only_flag` and frame cropping, exactly the fields the specification uses to derive a stream's own coded width and height. `isoTrackAllowed` now requires that SPS-derived size to exactly equal the sample entry's own container-declared width and height, in addition to both already being within `maxDimension` - a mismatch, for whatever reason, is refused outright rather than guessed at.

**Why avc3, VP9 and AV1 are refused rather than bound the same way.** avc3 is allowed by the H.264 standard to carry its own parameter sets in-band, inside sample data this package never reads (and never will - decoding sample data is explicitly out of scope, see the original `videosafe` entry above), so there is no way to bound which SPS a decoder might switch to mid-stream; it is refused outright, the same as any other codec this package cannot bind. VP9 and AV1's own configuration boxes (`vpcC`, `av1C`) do not carry a video's coded dimensions at all - only the compressed bitstream itself does, which would mean parsing a frame or sequence header, i.e. partially decoding sample data. Rather than draw that line differently for these two codecs alone, both are refused, and so is every WebM/Matroska video track: VP8, VP9 and AV1 are the only video codecs WebM carries in practice, and none of their dimensions can be bound from the container or a codec-keyed configuration either. A WebM file with a video track is now always reported not playable - the existing safe fallback (open externally, or save) a genuinely unsupported codec already took, not a new failure mode. HEVC was never in `allowedISOVideoCodec` and still is not: adding it would mean writing and bounding an equivalent `hvcC`/HEVC-SPS parser, which nothing currently requires.

**Why reject a custom H.264 scaling matrix rather than skip it.** A small number of H.264 profiles (100, 110, 122, 244, 44, 83, 86, 118, 128, 138, 139, 134, 135 - Annex A's own fixed list) carry `chroma_format_idc`, bit depth and a `seq_scaling_matrix_present_flag` ahead of the fields this package needs. When that flag is set, skipping the matrix correctly needs a full `scaling_list` parser (each of up to 12 lists, 16 or 64 Exp-Golomb-coded deltas, with an early-exit rule of its own) this package does not implement; rather than risk misreading where the bitstream actually continues, an SPS that sets this flag is refused the same way an unparseable one is. This only affects a High-profile (or similar) encode that also declares a custom scaling matrix, which most real encoders do not use by default; everything else - Baseline/Main/Extended profiles, and High-profile streams using the default scaling matrix - parses and binds correctly.

**Bounding the parse itself.** Every field read is bounded before anything is allocated: `maxSPSLength` (256 bytes) caps avcC's own declared SPS length before it is read into memory, and `maxExpGolombPrefix` (16) caps how many leading zero bits a single Exp-Golomb field's prefix may declare, so a crafted SPS cannot make the bit reader compute an implausibly large value or run past its own small, fixed buffer. `avcDecoderConfigSPSDims` refuses anything other than exactly one declared SPS (`numOfSequenceParameterSets`): more than one is the same "which one does the decoder actually use" problem as avc3's in-band parameter sets, and this package only ever answers for the one SPS it can see. Emulation-prevention bytes (H.264's own `0x03` inserted after two `0x00` bytes) are stripped before any bit is read, the same way a real decoder's own RBSP extraction would, so a legitimate SPS containing two zero bytes in a row is never misparsed.

The fuzz target and bit-level edge cases (a custom scaling matrix, `pic_order_cnt_type` 1's own variable-length list, an Exp-Golomb prefix past the bound) live in `h264sps_internal_test.go`, an internal (white-box) test for the same reason `readfailure_internal_test.go` is: reaching them through a full MP4 fixture built byte for byte is possible but adds nothing a direct call to `parseSPSDims` does not already prove. The integration-level behaviour this review actually found - a container and its SPS disagreeing, more than one declared SPS - is instead proven black-box, through `Validate` itself, in `isobmff_test.go`'s `TestValidate_MP4SPSDimensions`.

## A conversation's preview is capped on every read, not only when a message first sets it

A pre-release security review found a conversation's own `Preview` column was the one sender-controlled text "`re-apply ingest caps to legacy rows on every read`" (7611e0b) missed: `scanConversationWith` already re-capped `Title` and `PreviewSender` on every read for exactly this reason, but returned `Preview` exactly as stored. `bumpConversation` (`store/messages.go`) sets it straight from a message's own `Text`; before `domain.MaxIncomingTextLength` existed, a message received from a modified client could leave a multi-megabyte preview in the database, re-sent in full on every `conversations.list` response and every `conversation.updated` event - a read, mute, pin or typing change to that chat - until a newer message replaced it, even though `ui/lib/Format.js`'s `previewText` only ever shows the first 200 characters of it.

`domain.MaxPreviewLength` (300 UTF-16 units - a small margin above the UI's own 200, enough for the sender-name prefix a group preview adds before it is cut) and `domain.CapPreview` join `MaxDisplayNameLength`/`CapDisplayName` as the same kind of cap, used the same way: `scanConversationWith` now re-caps `Preview` alongside `Title` and `PreviewSender` on every read, repairing a row stored before this cap existed the same way those two already are, and `bumpConversation` caps it at write too, so a release from now on never stores more than the cap in the first place. `refreshAfterDeletion` (`messages_delete.go`), which falls a conversation's preview back to its newest remaining message's own `text` column by raw SQL, was left without a matching write-side cap: it can still copy up to `MaxIncomingTextLength` (64 KiB) into `preview`, but the read-side cap in `scanConversationWith` catches that the same way it catches a genuinely legacy row, so no path still reaches the UI uncapped.
