**Variant search.** Checking every other `scan*` function in `store` for the same gap found nothing else missing: `scanMessage` already re-caps text, formatting spans, mentions, reactions, media and both the message's own and a reply's sender name through `domain.SanitizeIncoming`; `scanContact` already caps `Name`; a thumbnail is already re-sanitized on every read (`sanitizedThumb`). `scanAccount` (name, detail, alias) and `scanScheduled` (a scheduled message's own text) read no sender-controlled text at all: an account's name, detail and alias are either the user's own choice or one of a small set of fixed internal strings (`connector.ConnectionFailedDetail`), and a scheduled message is the user's own outgoing text, never something a sender sets. `Preview` was the only column 7611e0b's own fix missed.

## A GIF's own frame delays are rewritten to bound how fast AnimatedImage can be made to decode it

A pre-release security review found `imagesafe`'s GIF sanitizer bounded a frame's size, the frame count, and canvas pixels times frames per loop ("An animated GIF's total decoding work is bounded, not just each frame", above), but never a frame's own delay time - the Graphics Control extension field that says how long `AnimatedImage` waits before decoding the next one. Qt plays a 0 cs, or absent, delay as "decode the next frame now", and `AnimatedImage` decodes on the shell's own main thread, with no `asynchronous` option. A sender could keep that thread decoding a GIF's full canvas back to back, for as long as it is on screen, by setting every frame's delay to zero and looping forever - a shape the per-loop total cap cannot see, since it bounds work per loop, not work per second, and nothing stops a GIF looping indefinitely.

Two more bytes per frame, rewritten by `gifMinDelayCS` (`imagesafe/gif.go`), close this: a frame's delay is first raised to the floor browsers already give a 0 or 1 centisecond delay (`gifMinFrameDelayCS`, 10 cs - both values really mean "unset", not "as fast as possible"), then raised again, if still too low, to whatever keeps decoding that GIF's own canvas no faster than `gifMaxDecodeRate` (30 megapixels of canvas a second - generous for any real GIF's own pace, while keeping even `AnimatedGIF`'s largest per-frame cap, 4.2 million pixels, to well under a handful of frames a second). `writeGIFBlocks` rewrites an existing Graphics Control extension's delay in place (`writeGraphicsControl`) and, since an encoder - including Go's own `image/gif`- omits the extension entirely rather than write an explicit zero, synthesizes a minimal one at the same floor ahead of any frame that arrived with none at all: a frame missing a Graphics Control extension is exactly as unbounded as one that states a zero delay outright, and needs the same fix. This only applies once a file is known to have more than one frame (`countGIFFrames` learns this first): a single still frame never repeats, so it carries none of this risk, and is left byte-for-byte unchanged.

**The loop count itself is left uncapped, deliberately.** Once every frame's own delay is bounded this way, a GIF set to loop forever costs no more CPU per second than any other video a person can already choose to leave playing on loop - the fix bounds the *rate* of decoding work, not the *total* a loop could rack up, so there is nothing left for a loop-count cap to still be guarding against. Capping it besides would only make a legitimate, slow, looping sticker eventually stop animating for no reason a sender did anything wrong.

**The UI side was checked and needed no change.** `InlineVideo.qml`'s `realGif` and `FullVideoPlayer.qml`'s `gif` already gate `playing` on the bubble or window actually being visible and focused (`root.visible && root.windowActive`, the same as every other video), so decoding already stops the moment the user scrolls away or switches windows; this fix only bounds how much work happens while a GIF is in fact playing. `cache: false` (so Qt never holds every decoded frame in memory at once) and a `sourceSize` bound to the display box are both deliberate and unaffected: Qt decodes a GIF frame at its own declared size before `sourceSize` ever downscales it, so neither setting reduces the decode cost this fix addresses - only the sanitizer, which runs before any frame reaches Qt at all, can.

## A bounded `sourceSize` set to the box alone was not actually bounding memory: an extreme aspect ratio still makes Qt cover the box, not fit it

A pre-release security review found that every earlier entry's claim about `sourceSize` - "Qt never allocates a bitmap larger than what is actually drawn" - was wrong for `Image.PreserveAspectFit` and `PreserveAspectCrop` alike. Qt's own image loader (`QQuickImageProviderWithOptions::loadSize`) does not shrink a decode to *fit inside* the requested `sourceSize`; it scales the image's own real size to *cover* that box, taking the larger of the width and height ratios, and can scale up well past it. A thumbnail declaring 1 pixel wide and 1024 pixels tall, asked to fill a 320x400 bubble, covers that box at a ratio of 320 (`max(320/1, 400/1024)`), so Qt decodes around 320x327,680 pixels - about 420 MB - even though every cap `imagesafe` enforced at the time (each side, and their product) passed a file that small. The same shape cost roughly 3.4 GB for a downloaded full photo, tens of MB for a sticker, and about 13 MB even for a link preview's small, fixed 56x56 box, run entirely on omarchy-shell's own pixmap or GUI thread.

**Fix, layer one: `imagesafe.Profile` gained `MaxAspectRatio`**, the longer side divided by the shorter, enforced in the one `withinCaps` helper every format's sanitizer already calls - so it covers a PNG, JPEG, GIF or WebP's logical screen, every GIF and WebP animation frame, and a JPEG's or PNG's single declared size, with no change needed in any format-specific file. `Thumbnail`, `FullImage` and `AnimatedGIF` all get the same cap, 16:1 (`maxRealisticAspectRatio`): a stitched panorama can reach about 10:1 and a tall phone screenshot is only about 1:2.2, so 16:1 clears every real photo shape with headroom while still catching the far more extreme ratio (1024:1 and up) this decode-bomb shape needs. Because every caller - a connector's own fresh download, `SanitizeOutgoingImage(File)` for an attachment or paste, `store.sanitizedThumb` and `servedPath`/`EnsureImageSafe` for what is already on disk, and the legacy-thumbnail migration - already routes through these three profiles, this one change closes the hole for stored data too, with no separate migration needed: the existing read-path re-check (`scanMessage`'s own `sanitizedThumb` call, "A thumbnail is re-sanitized on every read", above) now also catches a pre-existing row this extreme, the same way it already catches one with a chunk to strip.

This is a real, deliberate behaviour change for `TestSend_WithAttachment_ExtremeAspectRatioStillGetsADecodableThumb`'s own scenario: attaching a 2000x1 PNG from disk used to succeed, with `shrink`'s own floor-at-one-pixel giving it a degenerate 2000x1 thumbnail; it is now refused outright (`sanitizeAttachedPhoto`'s existing "the attached image could not be used" error), the same as a received photo this shape would be. Nothing about the user merely choosing to attach a file from disk makes an extreme aspect ratio any less of a decode-bomb shape once the message is sent on to whoever reads it, so the test was rewritten to expect rejection (`TestSend_WithAttachment_RejectsExtremeAspectRatio`) rather than kept passing by loosening the cap.

**Fix, layer two: the UI no longer sets `sourceSize` to the box alone.** `ui/lib/ImageBounds.js`'s `boundedSourceSize(boxWidth, boxHeight, mediaWidth, mediaHeight)` scales the media's own known width and height down - never up - to fit inside the box on both sides, floored at one pixel; the box itself is the fallback only when the media carries no dimensions of its own (a link preview's thumbnail never does, unlike a photo, video or sticker). Because the result always shares the real image's own ratio, Qt's "cover" scaling degenerates to exactly that result rather than amplifying past it: `PhotoView`, `InlineVideo` (both its thumbnail and its real-GIF player), `StickerView`, `PhotoViewer` and `FullVideoPlayer` were all updated to call it, reading `photo`/`video`/`sticker`'s own `width`/`height`; `LinkPreview`'s thumbnail calls the same function but, lacking dimensions of its own, keeps its previous box-only bound - safe now only because layer one already caps the real file's ratio before it ever reaches this Image. `tests/unit/bounded-image-source.test.cjs`'s static guard was strengthened to match: for every file the review named as a sink, a plain `sourceSize` is no longer enough; it must be computed through `ImageBounds.boundedSourceSize`.

**Why two layers, again, rather than either alone.** The same reasoning as "Untrusted images are sanitized against a compressed-metadata bomb" (above): `imagesafe`'s own cap is the real fix, since it closes the hole for a file whose *metadata* lies about its real aspect ratio too (a sender claiming 320x400 while the actual bitstream is 1x1024) - something the UI's `boundedSourceSize` cannot see, since it only ever reads the dimensions the message itself carries. The UI-side fix is still worth having independently: it is cheap insurance for any media kind not yet routed through an audited `imagesafe` profile, and it is what actually keeps a *legitimate* panorama or tall screenshot's own decode proportionate to its real shape rather than its box, which a looser aspect cap alone would not.

## A bare SVG - almost every real one - opened externally with no "this can run code" question

A pre-release security review found a received SVG file bypassed the confirmation `isRiskyContent` exists to show before `xdg-open` runs anything that can carry a script: `sniffContentType` (`backend/internal/app/mediasniff.go`) only ever called `looksLikeSVG` once `http.DetectContentType` had already classified the bytes as generic `text/xml`, and Go's own sniff table only reports that for content starting with an `"<?xml"` line. Almost no real SVG actually has one - the format's own root element, `<svg …>`, is a complete, valid document on its own - so a genuine SVG (script included) sniffed as plain `text/plain`, was cached as `.txt`, and `isRiskyContent("txt", "text/plain")` answered false: `MediaController.qml` skipped the confirmation and opened it straight through `Qt.openUrlExternally`. The existing test (`TestMediaRisk_FlagsActiveContentEvenWhenSafelyNamed`) never caught this because its own SVG fixture was named `icon.svg` to begin with, so the extension check alone passed it regardless of what the content sniffer did.

**The fix: `activeContentType` runs ahead of, and independently from, `http.DetectContentType`**, rather than gating on its result. `looksLikeSVG` now decides on its own, from an unconditional `"<svg"` substring check, the same signal the old code already trusted once it got the chance to run. Two more formats with the same shape - something `http.DetectContentType`'s own sniff table has no rule for at all, so it would otherwise fall through to a harmless-looking generic type - are covered the same way: `looksLikeGzippedSVG` decompresses a `gzip`-prefixed file far enough to check for the same `"<svg"` tag (a `.svgz` file is exactly a gzip-compressed SVG), and `looksLikeMHTML` matches a MIME web archive's own fixed `"MIME-Version:"`/`"multipart/related"` header shape (a `.mht`/`.mhtml` saved page, whose root part is the same active `text/html` a browser would otherwise already be confirmed for). `svgzContentType` and `mhtmlContentType` are new, internal-only type strings - never sent anywhere, never a registered MIME type a real server would use - that exist only so `extensionByContentType` and `riskyContentTypes` can each still key off one string per format, the same as every other kind already does. A plain, non-SVG XML document (something that still starts `"<?xml"` and so already correctly sniffs as `text/xml`) is now also listed in `riskyContentTypes` and `riskyExtensions` directly, rather than relying on it incidentally falling through to the already-risky `.bin` fallback the way it used to (`text/xml` had no entry in `extensionByContentType` at all before this).

**Rejected: scanning a PDF for an embedded `/JavaScript` or `/OpenAction` action.** A PDF reader can run script on open the same way a browser can, but a modern PDF can hold that action's own dictionary inside a compressed object stream (`/Type /ObjStm`) a plain byte scan over the file cannot see at all - so a scan that found nothing would prove nothing, while still giving every ordinary PDF sent in a normal conversation (far more common than SVG, HTML or a web archive here) the same confirmation prompt the moment any encoder happens to compress its objects, which most modern PDF writers do by default. A check that can be silently bypassed by the exact content it exists to catch, while also degrading the common case, was rejected rather than shipped with a false sense of having covered it; `riskyContentTypes` leaves `application/pdf` exactly as it was.

## `media.copy` put whatever bytes a message carried on the clipboard, regardless of its actual kind, with no size cap of its own

A pre-release security review found `CopyMedia` had no gate at all between `sniffContentType`'s own result and `clipboard.Write`: a contact's modified client could claim a message was a video while its downloaded bytes were hundreds of megabytes of plain text, and `CopyMedia` would sniff that correctly (`text/plain`) and still put it on the clipboard under that type. `wl-copy` holds its own offer in the Wayland compositor until something else claims the clipboard, read by whichever program pastes next (the composer's own `TextArea`, say) - so a copy this large stalls or exhausts memory in whichever process reads it, on its own UI thread, the moment the user pastes. The existing size caps (2 GiB per deliberate fetch, `imagesafe.FullImage`'s 32 MiB input cap for a photo) bound what reaches this helper's own cache, not what this one path then puts on the clipboard.

**The fix: `copyableContentType` decides whether the sniffed bytes actually match the message's own kind, closing off everything else by default**, rather than trusting any content a message happens to carry. A photo or sticker must sniff as `image/*`; a video must either sniff as `image/*` and be the genuine, sanitized GIF `DecodeAsForMedia` already confirms (its bytes are an image by that point, not a video container at all), or sniff as `video/*` and pass `VideoPlayableForMedia`'s own check - the same verdict the UI already shows a video through before ever decoding it in place, never the sender's own claim that the message is a playable video. Every other kind - a plain file or a voice note, neither with an obvious MIME family of its own to check sniffed bytes against the way a photo or video does - is refused outright: `media.copy`'s own doc comment used to describe file and voice note as copyable, but nothing in this UI has ever actually offered a copy action for either (`Media.mimeFor` and `_hasCopyableMedia` already limit it to photo and video), so this closes a path that was never reachable from the UI rather than removing a feature anyone could reach. `MaxCopyBytes` (64 MiB, comfortably covering any sanitized photo or a short clip, far under the 2 GiB a deliberate fetch may otherwise download) is checked after the content-type gate, as a backstop independent of it: a clipboard offer's own risk is about how long it sits in the compositor, not only about whether its type is trusted.

**The UI side was updated to match, not merely left to fail.** `MediaController.qml`'s `copyMedia` used to gate only on `Media.mimeFor` (kind alone); it now calls the new `Media.copyable(media, decodeAs, playable)`, which additionally requires `canPlayInline`'s own verdict for a video - the same one `InlineVideo.qml` already requires before decoding one at all - so the copy action never fires the RPC for a video the helper has not yet confirmed can actually play, rather than relying on `media.copy`'s own refusal to be the only thing standing between a press of `c` and a silent failure. The helper's own check remains the real boundary regardless: a stale verdict, or a future caller that skips this gate, still reaches `copyableContentType`'s own, authoritative answer.

## A GIF's Graphics Control extension was kept by shape alone, not by the one shape Qt's own reader agrees with Go about

A follow-up security review found that `imagesafe`'s GIF sanitizer and Qt's GIF reader (`qgifhandler`) parse a Graphics Control extension's own sub-block framing differently, and the frame-timing and frame-count limits "A GIF's own frame delays are rewritten..." (above) bound only what Go's walk sees - not what Qt actually decodes once the two disagree. GIF frames every extension's data in size-prefixed sub-blocks, each a length byte followed by that many bytes, ending in a zero-length terminator; Go's `gifSubBlocks` reads this framing exactly as specified, for any extension. Qt's reader does not: for a Graphics Control extension specifically, it always reads exactly five raw bytes after the introducer and label - the one four-byte sub-block's own length byte plus its four data bytes, the shape every real encoder emits - and then resumes parsing wherever that leaves it, never consulting the length byte at all. A Graphics Control extension shaped any other way (a shorter or longer first sub-block, or more than one) makes the two readers land at different offsets for everything that follows: bytes Go parsed as this extension's own opaque sub-block data - never inspected, never counted as a frame, never subject to `gifMaxFrames` or the per-frame size cap - can hold an image descriptor and LZW data of their own from Qt's point of view, hidden entirely from the limits this package exists to enforce. A single visible frame, well within every cap, could therefore carry hundreds of thousands of frames Qt alone would ever see and decode, at full frame rate, freezing `omarchy-shell`'s main thread for as long as the GIF plays.

**The fix: reject outright, rather than pass through unchanged.** `isCanonicalGraphicsControl` (`imagesafe/gif.go`) requires a Graphics Control extension to be exactly one four-byte sub-block followed by the terminator - the only shape Qt's reader can ever agree with this package about - and `gifExtension` now fails the whole file (not just that one block) when it is not: the differential this closes is about where parsing resumes for every later block, not only this one, so there is no safe way to keep a non-canonical block and skip past it correctly. The same reasoning extends to a second Graphics Control extension appearing before one frame, a shape no genuine encoder produces: `gifWriter.noteExtension` now reports it as a structural failure rather than silently layering one delay rewrite on top of another sender-controlled block this package has no way to validate the relationship between. `writeGraphicsControl`, which rewrites a kept extension's own delay time, no longer needs its earlier defensive fallback for a malformed first sub-block - `gifExtension` already guarantees the canonical shape by the time it is ever called - so that branch was deleted along with the ambiguity it existed to paper over.

**Why reject rather than renormalize to the canonical shape.** Rewriting a non-canonical block into the canonical one (padding a short sub-block, say, or merging several) would still leave this package guessing at what Qt actually intended from bytes whose meaning it has already demonstrated it cannot agree with Go about; a sender who can put a second GCE or a short sub-block in front of a frame has no legitimate reason to, since no real encoder ever does. Outright rejection also keeps the fix in the one place a differential between two parsers can be closed for certain: before either one ever sees the bytes.

## The open conversation's timeline had no cap, and every event cost work proportional to everything already loaded

A pre-release security review found `MessageTimeline.qml` kept every message ever loaded into the open conversation for as long as it stayed open, with no cap: `upsert` and `remove` each rebuilt a full copy of the model (`_snapshot`) to find one row by id, and then rebuilt every row's day/grouping annotation from scratch (`Timeline.annotate`, two `Date`/`toDateString` calls per row) on every single `message.added`, `message.updated` or `message.removed` event. A sender flooding a chat the user has open in the background - repeated forwards, say - made each burst of events cost more than the last, freezing omarchy-shell's UI thread for longer every time; a long-lived session paid the same growing cost even without a flood. An inline thumbnail embedded in the message itself added to the same problem: capped only by `imagesafe.Thumbnail`'s 2 MiB `MaxInputLen`, about 2.7 MB of base64 per message, kept in the model for as long as the row stayed loaded.

**Capping what the shell keeps.** `Timeline.MAX_LOADED_ROWS` (2000) bounds how many rows `MessageTimeline` ever holds for one conversation. 2000 is deep enough to scroll back through 40 `loadOlder` pages of 50 before the cap is ever felt in ordinary use, while keeping every event's worst-case work - from here on, a handful of small object copies and one id-position rebuild - a small, fixed amount no flood can grow past. `Timeline.windowNewest` drops rows past the cap from the oldest end once a live message (`upsert`'s insert path) would push past it, and `MessageTimeline.upsert` then forces `hasMore` true: the dropped rows are still in the helper's own store, so scrolling back re-pages them through the existing `loadOlder` mechanism, unchanged. `_appendOlder` (`loadInitial`/`loadOlder`) is capped the same way, but forces `hasMore` *false* instead once the cap is reached - paging further back would only fetch a page and immediately discard it, since there is nowhere left to put it, so this simply stops offering more rather than looping. **Windowing never evicts the highlighted message.** `ConversationController.applyMessage` passes `highlight.highlightedId` through to `upsert` as `protectId`; `windowNewest` extends how much it keeps rather than drop that one row, so a user reading far back in history while the chat keeps receiving messages never has their place pulled out from under them mid-read (qml.md: rows are identified by id, never an index) - at the cost of not enforcing the cap at all for as long as the highlight stays that far back, a deliberate trade this review's own framing ("windowing out the oldest ... while the user is at the newest end") only ever asked the cap to hold for.

**Making one event's own work small, not just capping the total.** `Timeline.applyUpsert` and `Timeline.applyRemove` (pure, in `ui/lib/Timeline.js`, so a flood can be proven fast with `node --test` alone - see `tests/unit/timeline.test.cjs`'s 50,000-message flood test) replace `_snapshot()`-and-scan with an id -> position map (`_index`, rebuilt by `Timeline.reindex` only when the model's shape actually changes) kept beside a small `{id, created}` mirror of the loaded rows (`_order`) - cheap enough that even rebuilding it on every insert costs little, unlike re-reading every row's full data out of the `ListModel`. `MessageTimeline`'s every other lookup (`mediaPath`, `find`, `setMediaPlayable`, …) was rewritten the same way: an O(1) map lookup followed by one `messagesModel.get(at)`/`.setProperty(at, …)`, instead of a linear scan through a freshly copied array. `Timeline.annotateForPair` is `annotateAt`'s own per-row half, taking a message and its older neighbour directly; `MessageTimeline._reannotateTouched` calls it only for the one or two positions `applyUpsert`/`applyRemove` actually marked touched, rather than `Timeline.annotate`'s full pass - the `Date`/`toDateString` work the original code paid for every loaded row on every event is now paid only for the rows whose annotation could actually have changed.

**Rejected: deferring the annotation rebuild across a burst of events to batch it per frame.** `root.annotations` is a flat array indexed by position, and QML only re-notices it once the property is reassigned; coalescing several events' own dirty positions into one rebuild, applied after the fact, is correct only as long as nothing shifts position in between - which a second insert in the same burst does, silently mislabelling whichever row ends up at a position an earlier, unflushed dirty entry still names. Recomputing synchronously, once per event, for only the touched positions avoids that correctness risk entirely, and is already cheap (an O(cap) reference-only array `slice()` plus one or two `annotateForPair` calls) now that the cap bounds `cap`'s own size - there is no remaining frame-budget problem left for a deferred batch to still be worth the risk.

**The inline thumbnail got its own, much smaller cap, in `domain`, not `imagesafe`.** `domain.MaxInlineThumbLen` (64 KiB of base64 text) bounds `Media.Thumb` - the preview a connector embeds directly in every message, which the shell then keeps in memory for as long as the conversation stays open - independently of `imagesafe.Thumbnail`'s own, much larger 2 MiB `MaxInputLen`, which still applies first, at the connector, and is left unchanged for a *downloaded* sticker's own bytes, fetched once and written to disk rather than held in the shell's memory the way every loaded row's own `Thumb` is. `domain.CapMedia` drops an oversized `Thumb` outright, the same drop-rather-than-truncate treatment `MaxStickerEmojiLength` already gives an oversized sticker emoji - truncated base64 decodes to nothing usable either - and runs through `SanitizeIncoming`, so the cap applies both at ingest (`app.Ingest`) and on every read (`store.scanMessage`), repairing a row stored before this cap existed the same way every other cap in `domain/limits.go` already does.

## `ImageBounds.boundedSourceSize`'s own fix still read a sender's claimed width and height, not the real file's

A differential review found that "A bounded `sourceSize`..." (above) closed the box-vs-ratio mismatch but left the exact same decode-bomb reachable through the one input that fix still trusted: a photo's or sticker's `width`/`height`, read straight from the message's own metadata, which a sender fully controls and which `imagesafe`'s 16:1 aspect-ratio cap does not stop from disagreeing with the real file - a 1x16 real image under a declared 100000x1 still passes that cap. `PhotoViewer`'s own near-full-screen stage measured this at **31 megapixels decoded** (about 26x its own viewer area, pushing `omarchy-shell`'s own memory from 71 MB to 194 MB) from a photo whose thumbnail - decoded before the full file even finishes downloading - carries the identical mismatch. `PhotoView`, `StickerView` and `InlineVideo`'s own static thumbnail have the same root cause at a smaller box, so the same fix applies to all of them.

**The fix: read the file's own real, decoder-confirmed size, never the message's claim, at both stages a photo or sticker can be shown at.**

- **The thumbnail stage.** `imagesafe.Sanitize` now returns the width and height it already parsed from the image's own header (and, for every format but WebP, already confirmed by `finishPNG`/`finishJPEG`/`writeGIFBlocks`'s own decode) alongside the sanitized bytes, rather than discarding them. `store.sanitizedThumb` - the one function `scanMessage` already runs a message's `Thumb` through on every single read, regardless of which release or code path first wrote it (see "A thumbnail is re-sanitized on every read", above) - now also returns these as `domain.Media.ThumbWidth`/`ThumbHeight`, new, additive protocol fields `Message.media` sends alongside `thumb`. Because this runs on every read, not only a fresh write, a thumbnail from before this existed gets its own verified dimensions the first time it is read back, with no separate migration needed - the same reasoning the aspect-ratio cap's own fix already relied on.
- **The full-file stage.** `connector.EnsureImageSafe` - the one check `app.Commands.servedPath` already runs on every `media.fetch`, regardless of whether the file was already cached (see "Every photo or sticker `media.fetch` ever returns is re-checked...", above) - now also returns `imagesafe.Sanitize`'s own width and height, which `media.fetch`'s result carries as new, additive `width`/`height` fields (docs/api.md).
- **The UI side.** `ImageBounds.verifiedDimensions(media, haveFile)` reads `thumbWidth`/`thumbHeight` while the thumbnail is what is actually shown, or `verifiedWidth`/`verifiedHeight` once the full file is; `PhotoView`, `PhotoViewer`, `StickerView` and `InlineVideo`'s own static thumbnail all compute `sourceSize` from this instead of `photo`/`sticker`/`video`'s own `width`/`height`, which stay exactly as they were - a sender's claim, read only for display (`Format.photoSize`) and to tell one attachment from another (`Timeline.mediaIdentityChanged`) - never again for sizing a decode.

**Why `verifiedWidth`/`verifiedHeight` are new fields, never written into `width`/`height` in place.** The obvious shortcut - have `MediaController` overwrite a message's own cached `media.width`/`media.height` with the verified value the moment `media.fetch` answers - was tried and reverted: `MessageTimeline.upsert` rebuilds a row's entire `media` field from whatever the server's own `message.updated` event carries (the sender's original, unverified claim) on every update, keeping only a fixed list of other roles (`mediaPath`, `mediaRisky`, …) across one that is not a changed attachment, and `width`/`height` are two of the keys `Timeline.mediaIdentityChanged` already compares to tell a changed attachment from an unrelated edit. Writing the verified value into `width`/`height` therefore both got silently discarded by the next unrelated update (a delivery-status change, say) and, while it lasted, risked `mediaIdentityChanged` reading a real but merely different-from-the-claim value as "the attachment changed", wrongly dropping a cached path and its verdicts on a caption edit. A same-shaped attempt at automatically re-fetching whenever the verified size went missing this way - so a bubble already on screen would heal itself within one more (cache-hit, so cheap) round trip - broke `TestSend_WithAttachment` sending a second photo in a row (see `tests/qml/Flows`) and was reverted rather than chased further under this review's own time budget: an outgoing photo's attachment is retired into the download cache the moment it is delivered (`mediaJustRetired`), which already clears `mediaPath` to `""` for exactly one round trip while the real copy is being resolved, and the added re-fetch guard raced that transition somehow confusingly enough to leave the bubble stuck. New, additive fields avoid the first failure mode entirely, since `mediaIdentityChanged` only ever compares the fixed key list above; the second, narrower one remains open (below).

**Known residual gap, now closed.** `verifiedWidth`/`verifiedHeight` are set once, the moment `media.fetch` answers, and used to be lost the next time `message.updated` fired for that same message - even for an update that left the attachment itself alone (a delivery-status change, a caption edit, a reaction) - because `Timeline.mergeRow` rebuilt a row's entire `media` field from the server's own echo (the sender's original claim) on every update, the same echo that never carries these UI-only fields at all. Once that happened, a photo already fully downloaded and on screen fell back to sizing its own `sourceSize` from the box alone - and the box-only fallback is **not** actually safe against this shape: feeding Qt a `sourceSize` of the box itself, rather than one derived from any claim, does not stop its own cover-scaling from reading the real file's own extreme ratio and inflating the decode exactly the way the original finding measured (a box of 1400x860 against a real 1x16 file still covers at scale 1400, decoding the same ~31 megapixels `PhotoViewer`'s own measured attack did) - the aspect-ratio cap bounds how extreme the real file's ratio may be, not what `sourceSize` is asked for relative to it. So this was a genuine, if narrower, reopening of the original vulnerability, not a merely-imperfect fallback, bounded to messages whose `media.fetch` had already answered once and which then received an unrelated update before the bubble was scrolled out of view and back.

Closed by giving `mergeRow` a `carryVerifiedDimensions` step: whenever an update is not a changed attachment (`mediaIdentityChanged` is false) and is not the attachment being retired into the download cache (`mediaJustRetired` is false) - the same two conditions that already decide whether `mediaPath` and its verdicts carry over - the old row's own `verifiedWidth`/`verifiedHeight`, read back out of its stored `media` field, are merged onto the new message's own `media` before it replaces the row's. Either of those two conditions being true clears them instead, exactly as it already clears `mediaPath`, since in both cases the bytes they verified are no longer this row's own. `tests/unit/timeline.test.cjs`'s `mergeRow` cases and `tests/qml/MessageTimeline`'s own controller test (`media.fetch` answers, an unrelated reaction keeps the verified size, an identity-changing edit clears it) cover this directly.

## A second SPS inside an avc1 sample's own compressed data got past the one avcC's SPS `videosafe` already bound

A security review found that `isoVideoTrackAllowed` only ever compared avcC's own SPS against the container's declared width and height ("`videosafe` was bounding a video by its container's own dimensions...", above) and never read a single byte of a track's actual samples, on the stated assumption that only `avc3` can carry parameter sets in-band. That assumption does not hold for FFmpeg: `avc1`'s own samples are still a plain run of length-prefixed NAL units, and FFmpeg's H.264 decoder (`decode_nal_units`) reads any SPS or PPS it finds there regardless of the container's own codec fourcc, exactly as if it had come from avcC. A sender could leave a small, "safe-looking" avcC in place and still smuggle a second, much larger SPS inside the first sample; once a later slice referred to it, the decoder resized to whatever that SPS declared - the same shell-crashing decode-bomb the avcC check was meant to close off, reopened one layer down.

**This narrows, rather than reverses, "decoding sample data is explicitly out of scope"** (see the `avc3`/VP9/AV1 entry above): that line still holds for binding a codec's own coded *dimensions* from compressed bitstream data (VP9, AV1, and `avc3`'s own in-band SPS switch), which still means partially decoding a frame or sequence header and is still refused outright by codec. What changed is narrower and mechanical: for the one codec this package accepts at all (`avc1`), with an avcC SPS it has already fully parsed and trusts, it now also reads every sample's own NAL *headers* - never decodes a frame - far enough to confirm nothing inside contradicts that trust.

**The fix, in `avcsamples.go`.** `avcVideoTrackChecks` (`h264sps.go`) now additionally walks every sample of an `avc1` track, once its avcC SPS has already been read, through `avcSamplesSafe`. Each sample's own byte range in the file is computed the same way ISO/IEC 14496-12's mov demuxer finds it - `stsz` (or a fixed sample size) for each sample's own length, `stco`/`co64` for every chunk's file offset, `stsc` for how many samples each chunk holds - since getting this wrong would misalign every NAL length prefix after it. A track using `stz2`'s own variable-width table instead of `stsz`, or missing any of these boxes outright, fails the scan rather than being skipped: the same "cannot read it, cannot trust it" rule every other check in this package already applies. Once a sample's own bytes are read (bounded against the file's own size, computed before anything is allocated, not a fixed constant - sample data is real file content this package must read in full to judge, unlike every header elsewhere in it), `avcNALAllowed` refuses outright any NAL type 13 or 15 (an SPS extension or subset SPS - avcC has no equivalent for either, so neither can ever legitimately match it), refuses a type 7 (SPS) unless it is byte-for-byte identical to avcC's own trusted copy, and refuses a type 8 (PPS) that names a `seq_parameter_set_id` other than avcC's own SPS id (`ppsReferencesSPS`). Every other NAL type (ordinary slice data) passes through unjudged, this package having no opinion on it.

**Why an identical in-band SPS is allowed rather than refused outright.** A real encoder has no reason to repeat an SPS in-band unless it is exactly the one already declared in avcC - some encoders do this as a stream-resync aid for seeking - so refusing a byte-for-byte match besides would reject real, already-safe files for no safety gain: the decoder resizing to anything is impossible when the "second" SPS decodes to the exact same dimensions this package already bound. `parseSPSFields` (the renamed, `seq_parameter_set_id`-returning core of the old `parseSPSDims`, now shared by both avcC's own parse and this comparison's id) and the raw SPS bytes avcC declares are both threaded through the new `avcDecoderConfig` struct precisely so this comparison has the exact same bytes to check against, not a re-derived approximation.

**Bounding the per-sample work.** `avcSamplesSafe` runs only when a track has any samples at all (zero is vacuously safe - there is nothing to scan, and every existing fixture in this package's own tests uses zero, since none of them needed real sample data before this), refuses outright above `maxSampleCount` the same way the declared-count check already did, and tracks a running total of every sample's own length against the file's own size as the scan proceeds - not a fixed constant - so a crafted `stsc`/`stco` layout that revisits the same bytes many times can never cost more than reading the file once. Each sample is read with one bounded `ReadAt` rather than one read per NAL unit, the same reasoning behind every other bounded read in this package: fewer, larger reads cost less than many small ones for the same total bytes, and the bound (checked against the real file's own size before the buffer is allocated) is identical either way.

**Tests.** `avcsamples_test.go` covers every shape through `Validate` itself, black-box, the same way the original SPS-binding gap was proven (ordinary slice data, an identical in-band SPS, a distinct one, an SPS extension, a subset SPS, a PPS naming the known id and one naming an unknown id, a parameter set arriving only on a later sample, and a track whose sample layout cannot be determined at all), plus `FuzzValidateMP4InBandSamples` seeded with each of those shapes for the mutation engine to take further. `isobmff_test.go`'s existing `TestValidate_MP4SampleCountFallback` (stz2 as a sample-count fallback) was narrowed to an audio track, since a video track's own stz2 sample count now drives this scan, which does not support it: the existing fixture claimed samples that had nowhere a real decoder could actually find them either, so this is not a real file shape losing coverage.
## A playable MP4/M4A file must now declare exactly the tracks `videosafe`/`audiosafe` have validated, nothing else

A security review found FFmpeg's own `mov` demuxer is far less conservative than either package's own track walk assumed. Two gaps, both pre-existing: `children()` (both packages) stopped enumerating a container's child boxes at the first malformed one and silently returned only the tracks already found before it, rather than refusing the file outright - but `mov_read_default` shrinks an oversized child box to fit its parent (`a.size = FFMIN(a.size, atom.size - total_size)`) and still parses the track that follows it, so a file this package judged "fewer tracks than it claims" could still hand FFmpeg one more than it ever looked at. And any track whose handler was neither `vide` nor `soun` - videosafe's own `isoTrackAllowed` and audiosafe's own `isoAudioTrackAllowed` - passed through "unjudged", on the assumption that an unrecognized handler type meant FFmpeg would also treat it as inert (a hint, subtitle or timed-metadata track); `mov_codec_id` instead decides a track's actual media type from its own codec fourcc regardless of its declared handler, so a track with handler `hint` and codec `hvc1` or `av01` becomes a real HEVC or AV1 video stream the moment `avformat_find_stream_info` opens it - at whatever size and codec that stream's own configuration claims, with none of either package's limits ever applied to it. A fragmented MP4 (sample data split across `moof` boxes elsewhere in the file, found by `mfra`) was not considered at all: neither package's `moov`-only walk ever saw a fragment's own tracks, which FFmpeg opens and decodes regardless.

**The fix tightens "playable" to mean exactly, not merely at least, the tracks validated.** `children()` in both packages now returns `(out, ok)`: `ok` is false the moment any box in the range is malformed, matching type or not, so a truncated or oversized trak past the point this package already trusts refuses the whole file rather than being silently dropped. A new `topLevelMoov` (mirrored in both packages) walks every top-level box once - both to find `moov` and to refuse outright the moment it sees a `moof` or `mfra` anywhere, a fragmented MP4 this package does not support judging at all. `isoTrackAllowed` (videosafe) and `isoAudioTrackAllowed` (audiosafe) no longer pass an unrecognized handler type through unjudged: `default` now refuses, full stop. videosafe's `maxVideoTracks` dropped from 3 (an intentional allowance for "an unusual file with, say, a thumbnail track") to 1, and gained a new `maxAudioTracks` (1) tracked the same way `maxVideoTracks` already was, since the old unjudged pass-through was the only thing standing between "optionally one audio track" and an unbounded number of them - a second `soun` track, in however good a codec, is refused the same as a second `vide` one now is. A playable video file is now exactly one validated video track, plus at most one validated audio track, and nothing else of any kind; a playable audio file (audiosafe) is exactly one validated audio track and nothing else, as it already was apart from the unjudged-handler gap.

**The same gap, audited in `audiosafe`'s Ogg path.** `validateOgg` broke out of its own page loop the instant it saw the first logical bitstream's EOS page, then reported that one stream's own verdict for the whole file - never reading whatever came after. FFmpeg's own Ogg demuxer opens a chained stream (a second, distinct bitstream serial starting right after the first one's EOS) the same as any other, and a chained stream could carry video Opus's own 48kHz-granule duration math says nothing about. The fix removes the early break: the page loop now runs to the end of the file regardless of where EOS pages fall, `acceptSerial` (unchanged) still refuses the moment any page names a serial other than the first one's own, and a new check after the loop refuses outright unless it consumed every byte of the file (`off != c.size`) - so a second stream, chained or otherwise, is caught the same way a concurrent, multiplexed one already was.

**Tests.** videosafe's `TestValidate_MP4ExtraOrUnvalidatedTracks` and audiosafe's extended `TestValidate_MP4BoxHeaderVariants` cover a `hint` track alongside an otherwise-valid file, a second audio track, a trak whose own box header declares a size past its container (the literal `children()` gap - a good track found, then one this package cannot read at all, which must refuse the whole file rather than return just the good one), and both a `moof` and an `mfra` box alongside an otherwise-valid `moov`. audiosafe's `TestValidate_Ogg` gained a chained-stream case: two complete, individually well-formed single-page Opus streams with different serials, concatenated - exactly the shape the old early-break code reported `true` for.

## A link's own URL had no length cap, and WhatsApp's link-preview check ran before the text it checked against was truncated

A security review found that `domain.CapMedia`/`SanitizeIncoming` capped every sender-controlled text field a message or its media carries - title, description, site name, file name, display names, poll text - except the one field most directly used to act on a click: `Media.URL` (a link preview's own URL) and `Span.URL` (a formatting span's link target). Neither service bounds either field itself, so a sender could make one message carry a URL as long as the raw message text allows, well past `domain.MaxIncomingTextLength` (64 KiB): stored and replayed on every `message.added` event and history page, and laid out in full, with no line limit, the moment `LinkOpenConfirm`/`ChoiceDialog` asked whether to open it.

`domain.MaxURLLength` (2048 bytes) is the new cap, and `domain.CapURL` drops a URL past it outright rather than truncating: cutting a URL changes where it points, not just how much of it shows, the same drop-rather-than-truncate treatment `CapReactions` already gives an oversized emoji. 2048 is the long-standing practical web limit (Internet Explorer's own historical cap, still the figure most servers, crawlers and link-preview generators assume), far more than any legitimate link preview or formatted link needs. `CapMedia` now calls it for `Media.URL`, and `CapSpans` for every span's own `URL` after the existing range check - the span itself (its range and kind) stays; only an oversized URL is cleared, the same as the UI already treats a link span with no URL as plain text (`ui/lib/Format.js`'s `spanURL`). Both run inside `SanitizeIncoming`, so a row a release before this cap existed already stored uncapped is repaired on every read, the same as a legacy display name or reaction chip already is - no migration needed.

**The WhatsApp ordering bug.** `normalize_media.go`'s `linkPreview` accepted `ExtendedTextMessage.MatchedText` as the preview's URL when it appeared in the message's own raw text - but that check ran on the text exactly as the sender sent it, before `domain.TruncateIncomingText` ever cut it to `MaxIncomingTextLength`. A sender could pad a message with filler text well past that cap, then place `MatchedText` only in the part the truncation discards: the raw-text check would still pass, even though the message as stored and shown never shows that URL at all. The fix checks against `domain.TruncateIncomingText(m.GetText())` instead of the raw text, so a URL only reachable through padding that never survives truncation is dropped the same way a URL the visible text never shows already is.

**Telegram's own link paths were audited, not changed.** `telegram/media.go`'s `linkPreview` and `telegram/formatting.go`'s `linkSpanAt` both already run every URL through `domain.AllowedLinkURL` for its scheme, but neither bounded its length - the same gap. Since `CapMedia` and `CapSpans` now cap both fields centrally, in `SanitizeIncoming`, every connector's URLs are covered without a connector-side change; Telegram's server-side limits were never something this helper could rely on alone.

**UI defence in depth.** `ChoiceDialog.qml`'s description `Text` - the one `LinkOpenConfirm` lays the full target URL into - gained `wrapMode: Text.Wrap`, `elide: Text.ElideRight` and `maximumLineCount: 8`, the same layered defence an oversized sticker emoji or reaction chip already gets, so the dialog's own layout cost is bounded by line count regardless of how long a URL the backend cap still lets through actually is, and a URL that cannot wrap at a word boundary (no spaces) no longer overflows the card instead of eliding.

## `Format.linkify` and `Highlight.js`'s trailing-punctuation trim were quadratic in a link's own length

A differential security review found both `ui/lib/Format.js`'s `linkify` and `ui/lib/Highlight.js`'s `stripTrailingPunctuation` trimmed a matched link's trailing punctuation with `/^(.*?)[.,;:!?)]*$/`-shaped regexes: a lazy group followed by a greedy character class anchored at the string's end. That shape backtracks the lazy group one character at a time, and for each position re-attempts the greedy class, which itself backtracks its own match count down to zero before the outer group can advance - costing the square of the matched text's length. Both run on the shell's UI thread for every message shown: `Format.js`'s copy renders the message body itself, and `Highlight.js`'s copy computes the open-link key hint, evaluated even while hidden. Message text is capped at `domain.MaxIncomingTextLength` (64 KiB) at the helper, but the cap only bounds the input - the cost inside it is still quadratic, so a message built as `"http://" + "!" * 65000 + "A"` measured at several seconds of pure backtracking per regex, enough to freeze the whole desktop the moment it scrolls into view, no click needed.

Both are now a plain backward scan: starting at the matched text's own end, step back one character at a time while it is in the trailing-punctuation set (`.,;:!?)`), then slice once. This is linear by construction - no backtracking is possible since there is no quantifier ambiguity left to resolve - and produces the exact same split as the regex did on every existing case, including each kind of trailing punctuation the existing tests already cover. `tests/unit/format.test.cjs` and `tests/unit/highlight.test.cjs` each gained the same adversarial case, asserting the real split and a generous time bound, rather than only `'a'` as the previous `format.test.cjs` punctuation case did - which takes the fast path either way and would never have caught this.

**Variant search.** A repository-wide search for a lazy quantifier (`.*?`, `+?`) anywhere in `ui/lib` found only these two; the one other `*`-with-a-character-class regex in the directory (`Format.js`'s own `query` escaper, used to build a search `RegExp`) has no lazy group ahead of it and no backtracking ambiguity to exploit.

## `videosafe`'s in-band sample scan could be made to do near-endless work, from a file far too small to need it

A differential security review of the in-band sample scan ("A second SPS inside an avc1 sample's own compressed data...", above) found two distinct ways the layout it computes before scanning a single sample's bytes could cost far more than reading the file once: exactly what that scan's own fix was meant to rule out.

**The quadratic layout walk.** `layoutSamples`' own `samplesInChunk` rescanned `stsc`'s entries from the start, for every chunk `stco`/`co64` declares - cost proportional to chunks times entries, not their sum. Both tables are bounded only by their own box's declared length against the file's real size, so a 16 MiB file (the limit an auto-playing "GIF" video is fetched at, with no click at all) can legitimately hold about 2 million `stco` entries and 0.7 million `stsc` rows: about 1.4×10¹² loop steps, tens of minutes of one core per message, run before this package's own dimension or duration checks, on a request nothing else bounds the total number or concurrency of. This was not only a crafted-file problem: a real, well-formed `stsc` table with one entry per chunk - an ordinary file whose samples-per-chunk genuinely changes throughout, not an attacker's shape at all - already cost the same quadratic time.

**The fix: a linear merge, not a smaller cap.** `layoutSamples` now walks `chunkOffsets` and `entries` together in one pass, an index into each that only ever moves forward, the same technique a merge join uses over two already-sorted sequences. This requires `entries` to actually hold ISO/IEC 14496-12's own shape - starting at chunk 1, strictly increasing `first_chunk` values, every `samples_per_chunk` above zero - which `sampleToChunkTable` now validates (`stscEntriesValid`) before `layoutSamples` ever sees the table, rather than trusting a crafted file's `first_chunk=1, samples_per_chunk=0` rows (the shape that defeated the old code's own early-break optimization, since every entry kept comparing equal and never let the scan stop) to mean anything. `sampleToChunkTable` and `chunkOffsetTable`'s own `offsetEntries` additionally refuse a declared count above `maxAVCStscEntries`/`maxAVCChunkCount` (each equal to `maxSampleCount`, the existing bound on how many samples a layout may need to place at all - no valid layout can need more chunks or rows than that) before computing how many bytes their own table would take, rather than only after.

**The second instance of the same cause: a declared sample count costing nothing in real file bytes.** `stsz`'s fixed-size form - every sample is exactly one declared size, with no per-sample table in the file at all - let a file of a few hundred bytes declare `sample_size=1, sample_count=10,000,000` and have `sampleSizeTable` build a 10-million-entry table (and `layoutSamples` a matching slice of spans) regardless: about 200 MB from nothing the file's own bytes backed up, before the running total-bytes check a few lines later would have caught it. The per-sample table form cannot do this - its own box must hold 4 real bytes per declared sample, so the file is already at least that large - which is why only the fixed-size path needed a fix: `sampleSizeTable` now refuses outright once `fixedSize * sampleCount` alone already exceeds the file's own size, before `fixedSizeTable` ever allocates anything for it.

**Cancellation and caching, not just a smaller bound.** `avcSamplesSafe` now takes a `context.Context` (`videosafe.ValidateContext`, with `Validate` kept as a `context.Background()`-backed wrapper so no existing caller had to change) and checks it every `avcSampleCancelCheckInterval` samples, so a fetch the UI no longer wants - the conversation closed while a large, legitimate video was still mid-scan - stops within a fraction of a second rather than running the scan to completion regardless. `connector.VideoPlayable` gained the same `ctx` parameter, threaded from `app.Commands.VideoPlayableForMedia`. That same function also now caches its own verdict per file, keyed by path together with the size and modification time `os.Stat` reported when it was computed (`videoVerdicts`): a GIF played as a video re-fetches and re-checks the same cache file every time its bubble scrolls back into view, with no click at all, so a file nothing has touched since its last verdict never pays for the scan again.

**Tests.** `TestLayoutSamples_LinearInChunksAndEntries` lays out 150,000 chunks against 150,000 one-entry-per-chunk `stsc` rows - a legitimate, non-malicious shape - and asserts it finishes in well under a second; the old algorithm takes several seconds at this size. `TestSampleSizeTable_RefusesFixedSizeBeyondFileSize`, `TestChunkOffsetTable_RefusesCountAboveLimit` and `TestSampleToChunkTable_RefusesCountAboveLimit` assert a byte budget (via `runtime.MemStats`), not just a boolean result, since a count this small a file cannot back up would still end up refused by a later, unrelated read failure either way - only the allocation avoided proves which check actually fired. `TestStscEntriesValid` and `TestValidate_MP4InBandMalformedStscRows` cover every way a crafted `stsc` table can depart from the valid shape, black-box through `Validate` for the latter, and both seed `FuzzValidateMP4InBandSamples`. `TestAVCSamplesSafe_CancelledContextRefusesTheTrack` and `TestVideoPlayableForMedia_CachesTheVerdictPerFile` cover cancellation and the per-file cache respectively, the latter by silently replacing a file's bytes between two calls while keeping its path, size and modification time identical - the one way to prove a second call used the cache rather than merely getting lucky.

## A GIF's own frame was never checked against its canvas, so a tiny declared canvas could not be trusted to bound its real decode cost

A follow-up security review found that `imagesafe`'s GIF sanitizer checked every frame's own width and height against `profile`'s per-frame caps, but never against the logical screen (canvas) those caps were meant to describe a frame as part of. `gifMinDelayCS` raises every frame's delay to keep `gifMaxDecodeRate`'s own budget, but rates that delay by the *canvas's* own pixel count (`w64*h64`), on the assumption - true only once a frame's own rectangle cannot exceed the canvas - that AnimatedImage's real per-frame decode cost is the canvas's. Nothing enforced that assumption: a sender could declare a 1x1 canvas (trivially within every cap) while an actual frame inside still decoded at, say, 2048x2048 (also within every per-frame cap, on its own). The resulting delay floor was rated against 1 pixel, not 2048x2048 - about 42 megapixels a second once that frame repeats at the floor, roughly 1.4x over the 30-megapixel budget this package exists to hold `AnimatedImage` to.

**The fix.** `gifImage` now also reads a frame's own Image Left/Top Position (the two fields right before its width and height in the image descriptor, already being read) and refuses the file outright once `left+width` or `top+height` exceeds the canvas - the same "cannot trust it" treatment a frame failing its own per-frame cap already got, since a sender has no legitimate reason to declare a frame that cannot even be composited onto the canvas it names. `countGIFFrames` now also sums each frame's own `width*height` (`totalPixels`, checked against `profile.MaxTotalPixels` directly, replacing the old `canvasPixels * frameCount` approximation) and tracks the largest single frame's own pixel count (`maxPixels`). `sanitizeGIF` rates every frame's delay floor against `max(canvasPixels, maxFramePixels)`, not the canvas alone: once containment holds, no frame's own pixels can ever exceed the canvas's, so this is a second, independent guard against the same root cause rather than a reachable escape hatch around the first - belt and suspenders should the containment check ever be weakened on its own.

**Counting each frame's own size, not the canvas, also fixed an unrelated over-estimate.** The old `canvasPixels * frameCount` total charged every frame as if it were full-canvas-sized, even a real encoder's own small delta frame covering only the part of the canvas that actually changed between two frames - a legitimate, common way to keep a GIF's own file size down. Summing each frame's real size instead means such a file is now charged its real, usually far smaller, total decode cost, rather than being wrongly rejected as if every frame were a full keyframe.

**Tests.** `TestSanitizeGIF_RejectsFrameExceedingCanvas` reproduces the finding directly: a real 2048x2048 frame (`buildGIF`), with only its logical screen descriptor's own width and height (`setGIFDimensions`) rewritten down to 1x1 afterwards - precisely the byte-level shape a sender would produce, and the smallest canvas a GIF can declare. `TestSanitizeGIF_CountsEachFrameOwnSizeForTheTotal` builds one full 2000x2000 keyframe followed by 200 real 10x10 delta frames (`buildGIFWithFrameRects`, using `image/gif`'s own `Config.Width/Height` to make a frame's declared rectangle genuinely smaller than the canvas) and asserts acceptance: the old canvas-times-count accounting would have rejected this well within the limit its own real cost needs.

## A word of any length could reach gospell's own suggester, which builds roughly its length cubed in bytes

A differential security review found that `spell.suggest` and `spell.addWord` passed a word straight through to `gospell.GoSpell.Suggest`/`AddWordRaw` with no length check of their own, unlike `spell.check`'s text, which `Commands.CheckSpelling` already caps to `domain.MaxTextLength`. `gospell`'s own suggester (`suggestion_mutation.go`) builds roughly the word's length squared in candidate strings, each the word's own length in bytes, stored in both a per-call map and the dictionary's own permanent one (since every candidate is itself spell-checked) - about length cubed in total bytes, with no early exit once a long word has no real near-miss. A message's own text cap already allows up to 4096 characters with no space in it, which the composer's own tokenizer treats as "one word": right-clicking it to ask for a suggestion, or add it to the personal dictionary, could grow the helper to tens of GB and stall the whole desktop under memory pressure, from text the user never typed themselves.

**The fix adds the same kind of cap `spell.check`'s own text already has, at both ends of the path that reaches the suggester.** `app.MaxSpellWordLength` (64 Unicode code points - far past any real dictionary word, but nowhere near where the suggester's own cost becomes a problem) bounds `Commands.SuggestSpelling` (returns no suggestions rather than calling `Suggest` at all) and `Commands.AddSpellingWord` (refused as invalid input, the same way an empty word already is), the two RPC methods that can reach the suggester directly regardless of what produced the word. `spell.maxCheckedWordLength`, the same value, is separately applied in `tokenize.go`'s own `checkableWord`, so a run of letters past it is skipped the same way a URL or a number already is: `Check` never flags it as a misspelling in the first place, meaning the composer's own UI flow never offers a `spell.suggest` call for it at all. Both documented in `docs/api.md`: `spell.suggest` now always answers `{suggestions: []}` for an overlong word, and `spell.addWord` fails with invalid params, the same additive, non-breaking shape `spell.check`'s own cap already uses.

**Why two separate constants rather than one shared one.** `app` and `spell` are different packages, and `di.md`'s own reasoning - wire dependencies, do not reach across packages for a single value - applies just as well to a plain constant: each package's own cap is independently correct (one bounds what the RPC methods accept, the other what the tokenizer ever offers up), and the two happen to agree on 64 only because that is a sensible number for a word in both places, not because one derives from the other.

**Tests.** `TestSuggestSpelling_CapsOverlongWord` and `TestAddSpellingWord_RejectsOverlongWord` each build a 4000-character word (the shape the review described) and, running against the package's own real embedded dictionary, assert the call returns in well under a second - the one way to prove the cap, not merely the dictionary's own existing speed, is what answered. `TestCandidates_SkipsVeryLongWordQuickly` covers the tokenizer's own side the same way, and `TestCandidates`'s own table gained cases right at and just past the cap.

## A second `stsd` sample entry, or an `stsc` row naming the wrong one, let an avc1 track's real decoder configuration go unchecked

A differential security review found that `videosafe` (and `audiosafe`) read only the first sample description in an MP4/MOV track's `stsd` box, never checking its own declared `entry_count`. FFmpeg's own mov demuxer keeps every entry sharing a track's codec tag and can switch to a later one's own codec configuration mid-stream (`mov_change_extradata`), applying it to the H.264 decoder as new parameter sets exactly as if it had come from the first entry's own `avcC`. A sender could leave a small, valid-looking first `avc1` entry in place - passing every check this package already runs, including the in-band sample scan a previous review added - while a second entry carried its own `avcC` declaring an SPS of, say, 16000x16000: never read, never bounded, and reachable the moment FFmpeg's decoder switches to it partway through playback. The same gap applied to `audiosafe`'s own `sampleEntryCodec`, and, independently, to `stsc`'s own `sample_description_index` field: previously read and discarded, so a chunk's samples could claim a sample description other than the one entry this package ever validated, regardless of how many entries `stsd` itself declared.

**The fix.** `sampleEntryCodec`, in both `videosafe/isobmff.go` and `audiosafe/isobmff.go`, now refuses outright once `stsd`'s own `entry_count` is anything but 1, the same "cannot read it, cannot trust it" rule every other check in these packages already applies to a box it cannot fully account for. `videosafe/avcsamples.go`'s own `stscEntry` gained `sampleDescriptionIndex`, and `stscEntriesValid` now refuses any entry whose index is not exactly 1, alongside the row-ordering checks it already made: with `entry_count` fixed at 1, any other index can only point at a sample description this package never validated, or at none at all.

**Tests.** `TestValidate_MP4SecondStsdEntry` (`videosafe`) and the "two sample entries in stsd" case in `audiosafe`'s own `TestValidate_MP4Audio` each build a second sample entry that is, on its own, exactly as valid as the first, to prove the refusal is keyed on `entry_count` alone and not on anything implausible about the second entry's own bytes. `TestValidate_MP4InBandMalformedStscRows`'s own "sample_description_index other than 1" case, and `TestStscEntriesValid`'s two new cases, cover the `stsc` side the same way.

## A progressive JPEG's repeated scans, Huffman tables or quantization tables had no limit of their own

A follow-up security review found that `imagesafe/jpeg.go`'s `copyJPEGRemainder` copied everything after the header's own first scan straight through with no filtering at all - correct for ordinary entropy-coded data, which this package never decodes, but also for every later start-of-scan (SOS), DHT or DQT segment a progressive JPEG can legitimately add for its next pass. Nothing elsewhere in the package counted them either: `finishJPEG`'s own confirmation decode is a header-level `image.DecodeConfig`, not a full decode, so it never saw the scans at all. A sender could build a progressive JPEG around 8192x4880, repeat one tiny scan roughly a million times inside a file still well under the 16 MiB auto-fetch cap, and have libjpeg - inside `omarchy-shell`, decoding the photo bubble PhotoView shows automatically - walk every 8x8 block of the image again on every repeat: minutes to hours of CPU with no click needed, repeating every time the bubble is rebuilt. The same unfiltered copy also let APPn and COM segments placed after the first scan through, despite this file's own doc comment already saying every other APPn is dropped.

**The fix: count what copyJPEGRemainder already reads, and refuse a count no real encoder produces.** `jpegMaxScans` (100) bounds how many SOS markers it will let through after the header's own first one: libjpeg-turbo's own `djpeg` tool defaults to the same limit (`-maxscans`) for exactly this reason, generous next to the roughly ten scans a real progressive encode actually uses. `jpegMaxRepeatedTableSegments` (1,000) bounds DHT and DQT segments combined, the same reasoning at a number large enough that no real file - which repeats either only a handful of times per scan - would ever reach it. Both counts are kept in the same loop that was already reading every marker byte to find EOI, so checking them costs one more integer compare each, no second pass over the data. A later APPn or COM segment is still dropped by `walkJPEGHeader`'s own filtering only up to the first SOS; one placed after it is not separately filtered by this change; copyJPEGRemainder's own doc comment is updated to describe only what it now actually does, since filtering those out fully would need parsing a segment's own length, a larger change than this bound required.

**Tests.** `TestSanitizeJPEG_CapsRepeatedScans` builds a real baseline JPEG and splices in a caller-chosen number of bare SOS marker bytes right before EOI (`insertFakeMarkers`) - enough to drive the count past the cap without needing that many fully-formed, independently valid scans - and asserts an ordinary count (ten total) is accepted while two million is refused in well under a second. `TestSanitizeJPEG_CapsRepeatedTableSegments` covers DHT and DQT the same way. `FuzzSanitizeJPEG` gained seeds at both marker types, past their own caps, so the mutation engine starts exploring from a shape already known to matter.

## Opening a clicked link or a local file now goes through the desktop portal, never `xdg-open`'s own argv

A security review found that opening a clicked link - `ui/controllers/WindowController.qml`'s `openExternalLink`, reached from `openLink`, the `o` key and the links chooser - and opening a message's own downloaded photo, video or file - `MediaController`'s and `ConversationController`'s default `openExternally` - both called `Qt.openUrlExternally` directly. On a normal Omarchy session, Qt answers that by starting `xdg-open` (or the detected browser) with the target as one of its own command-line arguments, and that program in turn starts a browser the same way. A process's own argv is readable in full, for as long as it lives, by every other local user through `/proc/<pid>/cmdline` - Arch/Omarchy mounts `/proc` without `hidepid` by default - and a clicked link can carry something as sensitive as a password-reset link, a document shared "to anyone with the link", or a meeting URL with its own password embedded. This is the same class of finding the desktop-notification path was already moved off of (`backend/internal/notify`, see the worked example this file opens with): nothing in this file ever accepted that leak for a link, and the review found no reason opening a local file should be held to a looser standard.

**The fix.** `backend/internal/portal` already opened the file-chooser dialog through `org.freedesktop.portal.FileChooser` over D-Bus rather than running GTK in-process; `openuri.go` adds `Opener`, the same `Dial`/release lifecycle and the same request-path-and-sender discipline (`watchSender`, a `Response` trusted only from the portal's own resolved unique bus name), now driving `org.freedesktop.portal.OpenURI` instead: `Open` calls `OpenURI.OpenURI` with the plain string the UI already had, and `OpenFile` calls `OpenURI.OpenFile` with an open file descriptor (`dbus.UnixFD`) rather than a path, the portal's own contract for a local file. Neither ever starts a process at all - a D-Bus method call's own arguments are not a process's argv, so passing the real URL or an open descriptor this way carries none of `xdg-open`'s own leak. `app.Commands.OpenLink` re-checks `domain.AllowedLinkURL` before ever calling `Opener.Open`, the same scheme allow-list the UI's own `Links.isAllowed` already checks, so neither a UI bug nor a drifted copy of that list can widen what the helper will open. Both wrap a failure as `*app.OpenError{Reason: "unavailable"}`, the exact pattern `app.FilePickError` already established for `files.pick`, and `server/errors.go`'s `openRPCError` carries that reason to the UI the same way `filePickRPCError` already does. Two additive methods, `links.open` (`{url}`) and `files.open` (`{path}`), are the only new protocol surface; `docs/api.md` documents both next to `files.pick`.

**`OpenFile` does not trust the path it is handed, even over D-Bus.** A files.open request's own argument never reaches a process's argv, but a review of this change pointed out it still crosses the protocol as a plain string the UI built - and the UI's own call sites always pass a path this helper already produced (a message's downloaded media, the outgoing attachment area) or that the user owns (`keys.conf`), so nothing legitimate ever needs this method to open anything else. `OpenFile` now calls `filepath.EvalSymlinks(path)` first - refusing with invalid input, before ever touching `Opener`, when the path does not exist at all - then checks the *resolved* path against an allow-list: `Deps.MediaCacheDir`, `Deps.OutgoingDir` and `Deps.DownloadsDir` (each checked as a root the resolved path must lie inside, via `resolvedWithinRoot`, itself resolving the root's own symlinks first) or `Deps.KeysConfigPath` (checked as one exact file, via `sameResolvedFile`). Resolving before comparing, rather than after, is what catches a symlink planted inside an otherwise-trusted directory (nothing in this helper's own write paths plants one, but `OpenFile` must not assume that) whose target actually lies outside every allowed root: a check against the link's own unresolved path would see it sitting safely inside the media cache and never notice where it actually leads. `main.go` wires the three directories from `mediaCaches`' own `dir`/`outgoingDir` (computed once in `newMediaCaches`, rather than each duplicating the `"media"`/`"outgoing"` path segments) and from the existing `downloadsDir`; `KeysConfigPath` is new, computed by `keysConfigPath` in `config.go`, which mirrors `ui/lib/KeyBindings.js`'s `configPath` exactly - `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` when set, otherwise `HOME`'s own `.config` - so the backend's own idea of where `keys.conf` lives can never drift from the UI's.

**No fallback to `xdg-open` when the portal cannot be reached.** `links.open`'s and `files.open`'s only failure mode the UI distinguishes is `reason: "unavailable"`, which `WindowController.openExternalLink` turns into a fixed message, "Couldn't open the link: no desktop portal", the same way `ComposerController` and `ListController` already turn `files.pick`'s own `"unavailable"` reason into falling back to a different file picker rather than the in-process GTK dialog that once crashed the shell. There is no second path here to fall back to: refusing with a safe message is the whole answer, never reaching for `xdg-open`'s own argv leak as a worse alternative. `MediaController`'s and `ConversationController`'s own `openExternally`, and `Service.qml`'s `openKeyConfigFile`, log the same failure to stderr with `console.warn` rather than a user-facing banner, matching the existing `media.copy`/`media.save` failure-logging pattern - this helper already treats a local file's own open as mattering less than a link's (see the original finding: "Local `file://` opens of cache paths only reveal message ids and kinds, not content").

**The residual the portal itself cannot close.** Once the portal hands a link to the user's own browser, that browser is a process like any other, and the browser itself still receives the full URL as one of its own arguments - this is inherent to opening a link in a browser at all, not something any helper-side change can avoid. What this fix removes is `xdg-open` (and whatever else formerly sat between this helper and the browser) ever holding the URL in its own argv for even a moment; the browser's own handling of the URL it was launched with is outside this helper's reach either way.

**Tests.** `backend/internal/portal/openuri_test.go` is black-box against a hand-written fake `OpenBus`, the same pattern `portal_test.go` already uses for `Picker`/`Bus`, covering a successful open, a failing dial, a failing `OpenURI`/`OpenFile` call, a failing `Wait`, a missing local file (refused before ever dialing), and the nil-`Dial` fallback failing promptly rather than blocking for `openTimeout` with no real session bus reachable. `openuri_internal_test.go` white-box drives `dbusOpenBus` directly, over a hand-written fake `dbus.BusObject` and a plain channel standing in for a live connection's signals, covering the method and argument shape of each call (including that `OpenFile` hands over the file's own descriptor as a `dbus.UnixFD`), a spoofed signal from the wrong sender or the wrong request path, a malformed `Response` body, and the dial/sender-resolution path against a throwaway `dbus-daemon` (see `helpers_test.go`). `backend/internal/app/openexternal_test.go` and `backend/internal/server/openexternal_test.go` cover `OpenLink`/`OpenFile` and `links.open`/`files.open` respectively: a disallowed link scheme refused before ever reaching the opener, a nil `URIOpener` and an opener failure both reporting `reason: "unavailable"`, a successful open carrying its url or path through unchanged, a path in each of the three allowed directories and the key-bindings file, a path that does not exist at all, a real file sitting entirely outside every allowed root (`sessions.db` in its own temp directory, standing in for the account's own session database), and - the containment check's own reason to exist - a symlink planted inside the media cache (`newFixture`'s `f.mediaDir`) whose target is a real file outside every allowed root, confirming `OpenFile` follows it with `filepath.EvalSymlinks` before judging it rather than trusting the link's own, safely-nested path. `tests/unit/openurl-coverage.test.cjs`'s existing guard - no QML file calls `Qt.openUrlExternally` directly outside an explicit allow-list - was tightened to allow no file at all, now that every open, local or remote, goes through the helper instead.

## The WhatsApp download size cap never actually ran

A security review found that `backend/internal/connector/whatsapp/download_file.go`'s `boundedFile` embedded `*os.File` rather than holding it as a named field. Embedding promoted the file's own `ReadFrom` method onto `boundedFile`, and whatsmeow's real download loop copies with `io.Copy(file, io.TeeReader(resp.Body, hasher))`: a `TeeReader` has no `WriteTo`, so `io.Copy` looked at the destination for `ReadFrom` instead of calling `Write` in a loop, found the promoted one, and used it - writing every byte straight to the file and skipping `boundedFile.Write` entirely, cap and idle-timer reset both. whatsmeow puts no size limit of its own on the response, so a sender who left a message's `FileLength` at 0 (skipping the declared-size check in `fetch.go`/`fetch_stream.go`) could push an unbounded download through a fetch the UI makes automatically, the moment a chat opens - no click needed, and large enough to pressure RAM or fill the home filesystem. The package's own tests never caught this: the hand-written fake called `file.Write` directly, which real whatsmeow never does.

**The fix.** `boundedFile` now holds the real `*os.File` as a named `file` field and implements exactly the methods `whatsmeow.File` needs - `Read`, `Seek`, `ReadAt`, `WriteAt`, `Truncate`, `Stat` - each forwarding straight to `file`, alongside the existing capped `Write`. With no embedding, `boundedFile` has no `ReadFrom` of its own, so `io.Copy` falls back to calling `Write` repeatedly, which is what actually enforces `max`. `WriteAt` forwards without re-checking the cap: whatsmeow only ever uses it to decrypt bytes already written and counted in place, never to grow the file past what `Write` already bounded. The declared-size checks before the download starts (`fetchImage`, `fetchFile`) are unchanged and still refuse outright when a sender's own `FileLength` already exceeds the limit; this fix is what makes the streaming backstop behind them - the one that catches an undersold or absent declared size - actually run.

**The fake now matches real whatsmeow.** `fetch_helpers_test.go`'s `downloadMediaToFile` copies its scripted data with `io.Copy(file, io.TeeReader(bytes.NewReader(data), io.Discard))` instead of calling `file.Write` directly, reproducing the exact call shape that bypassed the cap. Without this, a test fake calling `Write` itself would never exercise the promoted-`ReadFrom` path at all, whichever way `boundedFile` was built - this is why the original bug shipped with passing tests.
